It is a supposition that must be entertained, even though it is not a very good one. I believe that the facts will eventually prove the criminal to be a legitimate inmate.”

“That narrows the field, doesn’t it, Lieutenant, to whoever was in Mrs. Endicott’s room?”

“It does, unless somebody dropped a rope ladder from an upstairs window and got onto the balcony in that way. But I don’t put much stock in those tricks, Doctor, any more than I do in sliding panels and trapdoors. Outside of the badger game I’ve never come across a sliding panel in my life, and I don’t ever expect to, either.”

===Chinese===
“医生，”瓦尔考中尉说，“眼下我们最紧要的，是查清那一枪究竟是谁开的。最主要的理由说来未免书生气：我们要把开枪的人捉拿归案。次要的理由则是，许多人一旦精神失衡到受驱使去杀人的地步，往往不会就此收手。他们已经尝过了血腥。他们正处于一种异常亢奋的状态，被一种新的恐惧所驱使：那就是被发觉、被捕获的恐惧。他们盘算着，为了不被抓住，何不再杀一个？横竖也不会损失什么。您要知道，一个人只能被电椅处死一回。我当然是假设凶手是个外来者——某个此刻仍藏在这宅子里的人，会拼死设法脱逃。这个假设即便并不怎么高明，却也不得不纳入考虑。我相信，事实终将证明凶手是这屋里名正言顺的住户之一。”

“这么一来，中尉，范围就缩小到当时在恩迪科特夫人房里的人身上了，是吗？”

“确实如此，除非有人从楼上某扇窗子放下一条绳梯，沿着这条路登上阳台。可我不大信这些花样，医生，就像我同样不信什么暗板和翻板。除了美人局那一类的勾当之外，我这辈子还从未撞见过什么暗板，日后也不指望会撞见。”

===Original===
Dr. Worth was inclined to take the idea more seriously. “But a rope ladder—there might very well be one around the house for an emergency fire escape.”

“All right, who was in the room just above this one? You. Did you come down a rope ladder and shoot Endicott?”

“God’s truth—my dear man—”

“Oh, be sensible, Doctor, of course you didn’t. And who had the room across the hall from you, which also is above the balcony? Mrs. Siddons, the housekeeper. If you saw her, you’d scarcely picture her as hurrying up and down a rope ladder. No, Doctor, whoever was on that balcony came from Mrs. Endicott’s room. We’re back to the same three people: Mrs. Endicott, her maid, and her nurse.”

“But Mrs. Endicott is out of the question, Lieutenant. She is still under the influence of the narcotic I gave her.”

“How about the nurse, Doctor? Have you known her long?”

“Known her? Only for the several cases she has worked on with me. But she comes from the most reputable agency in the city. How about the maid?”

“I don’t know.”

“She is just as good a candidate for suspicion as Miss Vickers, isn’t she? Why under the sun should Miss Vickers want to shoot Endicott?”

===Chinese===
沃思医生倒更愿意把这想法看得认真些。“可绳梯——宅子里备一条作火警逃生之用，那也大有可能。”

“好罢，就在这间房楼上那间房里的是谁？是您。难道是您顺着绳梯下来开的枪，打死了恩迪科特？”

“老天为证——我亲爱的老兄——”

“哦，讲点道理，医生，您当然没有。那么，在您对门那间房里、同样也位于阳台正上方的，又是谁？是西登斯太太（Mrs. Siddons），那位管家。您只要见她一面，就断难想象她在一条绳梯上爬上爬下。不，医生，无论谁上了那阳台，都是从恩迪科特夫人房里过去的。我们又回到了同样那三个人身上：恩迪科特夫人、她的女佣，还有她的护士。”

“可恩迪科特夫人根本不可能啊，中尉。她仍在我给她服的安眠药力之下。”

“那么护士呢，医生？您认识她很久了吗？”

“认识她？只不过她同我合作过的那几个病例而已。可她来自全城口碑最好的那家介绍所。那么女佣呢？”

“我还说不准。”

“她和维克斯小姐（Miss Vickers）一样值得怀疑，不是吗？天下哪有半点道理，让维克斯小姐去枪杀恩迪科特？”

===Original===
“I’m not seriously considering Miss Vickers at all. It’s perfectly obvious that whoever did shoot Endicott was either directly responsible for the earlier attack during the evening or else involved in it as an accomplice.”

“That might still include the maid.”

“It certainly might. I wonder if you’d mind asking Miss Vickers to come in here. I’d like to question her first.”

Dr. Worth nodded toward Endicott’s body, covered with a sheet on the bed. “Miss Vickers, Lieutenant, being a nurse is naturally accustomed to seeing the dead, but it will be rather gruesome for the maid if you question her in here, too.”

“Very gruesome, Doctor.”

“Well, you know best. You’re liable to have a fine case of hysterics on your hands.”

“I’ll risk it.”

Dr. Worth left and closed the door. There again swept over Lieutenant Valcour, with the solitude, that indefinable feeling of some lurking dread. There were voices crying out to him from the subconscious, warning him of dangers that were very real, very close at hand—but the messages were indecisive, as are all instinctive things which fall beyond the charted seas of any human knowledge.

===Chinese===
“我压根儿就没把维克斯小姐认真算作嫌疑人。再明显不过，开枪打死恩迪科特的人，要么直接就是今晚早些时候那场袭击的元凶，要么便是作为同谋卷入其中。”

“那仍旧可能把女佣算在内。”

“的确可能。不知您介不介意请维克斯小姐到这儿来一趟。我想先盘问她。”

沃思医生朝床上那具盖着床单的恩迪科特的尸体点了点头。“维克斯小姐身为护士，中尉，自然见惯了死者，可您要是在这儿也盘问女佣，对她就未免太惨刻了。”

“太惨刻了，医生。”

“这个嘛，您最清楚。弄不好您手上会落下一场漂亮的歇斯底里大发作。”

“我愿冒这个险。”

沃思医生出去了，带上了门。随着四下的孤寂，那种说不清道不明的、有什么祸患正潜伏其中的感觉，再次涌上了瓦尔考中尉的心头。有一些声音从他潜意识深处向他呼喊，警告他所面临的危险是那样真实、那样近在咫尺——可这些讯息却模棱两可，正如一切越出了人类已测绘海域之外的、凭本能感知的事物。

===Original===
Nurse Vickers came in without the formality of knocking. Her glance toward the bed was professional and not coloured by any sign of nervousness.

“Thank you for coming, Miss Vickers. I’ll only bother you for a minute.”

“No bother at all, Lieutenant.”

“There is just one thing I want to know: who was in the room with you and your patient at the time of the shooting?”

“Why, I couldn’t say, Lieutenant, exactly.”

“Why not, Miss Vickers?”

“Because I wasn’t there myself. I was down in the kitchen making some coffee. I left Roberts with Mrs. Endicott. You see, there wasn’t anything that had to be done except just to be there. I’m sure it was quite all right.”

“Of course it was. I’m not suggesting for a minute, Miss Vickers, that I thought otherwise.” Lieutenant Valcour studied the woman for a second and then said, “I just wanted to know if you could help me check up on the number of shots that were fired.”

“I didn’t hear any shots at all, Lieutenant, ’way down there in that kitchen.”

===Chinese===
维克斯护士连门也没敲，就这么径直走了进来。她朝床那边投去的一瞥，是职业性的，不带一丝紧张的痕迹。

“多谢您过来，维克斯小姐。只打扰您一会儿。”

“一点也不打扰，中尉。”

“我只想知道一件事：开枪的时候，有谁和您、和您的病人同在那间房里？”

“这我可说不上来，中尉，说不确切。”

“为什么呢，维克斯小姐？”

“因为我自己当时并不在那里。我下楼到厨房煮咖啡去了。我把罗伯茨（Roberts）留下来照看恩迪科特夫人。您要知道，当时并没有什么非做不可的事，只要有人待在那儿就行。我敢说那样安排是完全可以的。”

“当然可以。维克斯小姐，我绝没有半点别的意思。”瓦尔考中尉打量了她一秒钟，然后说，“我只是想知道，您能不能帮我核实一下开了几枪。”

“我一枪也没听见，中尉，在那底下的厨房里压根儿听不见。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour wondered at this. The sound of one shot might well have been heard down in the kitchen: the shot which had killed Endicott and which had been fired from the balcony. The sound would surely have travelled clearly in the still night air and to the kitchen from outside. And yet he believed Nurse Vickers implicitly in her statement that she had heard no shot. There was no earthly reason why she should lie about it. The fact convinced him that whoever had fired had held the pistol inside of the window. He glanced at the sash and realized that the opening afforded plenty of room for a hand holding a gun to reach through.

“No,” he said, “I suppose you couldn’t have heard anything at all. Maybe Roberts can help me. She was in the room, wasn’t she, when you came back?”

“Oh, yes, Lieutenant, and terribly excited about the shooting. She seemed so upset, in fact, that if there hadn’t been so many much more important things for Dr. Worth to attend to, I’d have asked him to give her something to quiet her.”

“One can hardly blame Roberts,” Lieutenant Valcour said. “The fusillade must have been quite a shock, you know. And then everyone’s nerves are on edge tonight anyway. In just what fashion was she upset, Miss Vickers? From your professional experience, I mean, you probably could diagnose her actions. Was it fright—nervous shock?”

“Oh, fright, of course, Lieutenant. I’ve seen lots of nervous and hysterical people during my work but never one as badly off as she was. I’m not exaggerating one bit when I say that she was gripped with an hysterical sort of terror.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉对此深感纳闷。要听见那一声枪响，在厨房里其实是完全可能的：就是从阳台上射出、打死恩迪科特的那一枪。在那样寂静的夜气里，枪声从屋外传到厨房，定然传得清清楚楚。然而维克斯护士说她一枪也没听见，他却全然信她。她在这件事上没有半点撒谎的理由。这一事实使他确信：开枪的人，是把枪伸进了窗子里面才扣的扳机。他瞥了一眼窗扇，发觉那道缝隙完全容得下一只握枪的手伸进去。

“是啊，”他说，“我想您确实什么都听不见。也许罗伯茨能帮我。您回来的时候，她还在房里，是吗？”

“哦，是的，中尉，她对开枪这事激动得很。她看上去实在吓坏了，说真的，要不是还有那么多更要紧的事等着沃思医生去料理，我就会请他给她一点什么东西，好让她镇定下来。”

“这也怪不得罗伯茨，”瓦尔考中尉说，“那阵连珠似的枪响，想必是个不小的惊吓，您要知道。况且今夜人人都神经紧绷。她当时是怎么个闹法呢，维克斯小姐？我的意思是，凭您的职业经验，您多半能诊断她的种种举动。是惊恐——神经性休克？”

“哦，自然是惊恐，中尉。我干这一行见过的神经质和歇斯底里病人多了去了，可从没见过像她那样严重的。我说她被一种歇斯底里式的恐惧攫住，这话可没有半分夸大。”

===Original===
“Really. As bad as that?”

“Why, I was almost afraid even to let her stay in the room with the patient. The poor creature actually seemed to blame Mrs. Endicott in some fashion for what had happened. Just imagine this, Lieutenant: when I came in she was literally leaning over the bed and shaking her fist at Mrs. Endicott.”

“You are quite certain of this, Miss Vickers?”

“I saw it with my own eyes, Lieutenant.”

“And was Roberts saying anything?”

“Just the jumble that people go in for when they’re hysterical.”

“You couldn’t catch anything connected?”

“I didn’t try, Lieutenant. I had to get her away from the bed and calm her down.”

“You were able to?”

“I was. She calmed down quite suddenly and became perfectly normal again. I persuaded her to run downstairs and make herself a good bracing cup of tea.”

“Possibly carrying the pistol with her,” Lieutenant Valcour thought bitterly, “to hide it in some place where it might never be found.”

===Chinese===
“真的吗。严重到那个份上？”

“可不是嘛，我几乎都不敢让她继续和病人待在一间屋里了。那可怜的人，竟然好像把发生的事，多少怪到了恩迪科特夫人头上。您且设想一下这个场面，中尉：我进去的时候，她简直就伏在床边，朝着恩迪科特夫人挥拳头。”

“这事您相当确信吗，维克斯小姐？”

“我亲眼瞧见的，中尉。”

“那么罗伯茨嘴里有没有说什么？”

“无非就是人们歇斯底里发作时那一套东拉西扯。”

“您没听出什么连贯的话来？”

“我也没去听，中尉。我得把她从床边拉开，让她安静下来。”

“您做到了？”

“做到了。她相当突然地就安静下来，又完全恢复了正常。我劝她下楼去，给自己沏一杯提神的好茶喝喝。”

“说不定还顺手带走了那把手枪呢，”瓦尔考中尉苦涩地想，“把它藏到某个再也找不着的地方去。”

===Original===
“Did she come back into the room afterward?” he said.

“Well, not really, Lieutenant. I know how particular you police officers are about the littlest details. She just stopped at the door to tell me she was feeling all right again. She said she was going upstairs to her room to take a little rest.”

“And you’re quite sure, Miss Vickers, that you can’t recall any of the words that Roberts was saying when you found her leaning over the bed?”

“I would if I could, Lieutenant. It was just a jumble. Ice—something about ‘ice and human hearts.’ Then she switched to ‘searing flames’ and I don’t know what all else.”

“Would it bother you very much to go up to her room and see whether she’s in condition to come down here for a few minutes?”

“Why, not at all. I’d be glad to.”

“Thank you, Miss Vickers. You’ve helped me tremendously. Oh, there’s just one thing, Miss Vickers.”

Miss Vickers paused at the doorway.

“Yes, Lieutenant?”

===Chinese===
“之后她又回过这间房吗？”他说。

“呃，算不上回过，中尉。我知道您当警察的对最小的一点细节有多较真。她不过是在门口停了停，告诉我她又没事了。她说她要上楼回自己房里歇一小会儿。”

“那么维克斯小姐，您相当有把握，当您发现罗伯茨伏在床边时，她嘴里说的那些话，您一句也想不起来了？”

“能想起来的话我早想了，中尉。那纯粹是东拉西扯。冰——什么‘冰和人心的’。接着她又改口说什么‘灼人的火焰’，还有些什么我就不知道了。”

“那要麻烦您到她房里去看看，她是不是能下楼到这儿来待几分钟，会非常打扰您吗？”

“哪里的话。乐意之至。”

“多谢您，维克斯小姐。您帮了我大忙。哦，还有一件事，维克斯小姐。”

维克斯小姐在门口停了下来。

“什么事，中尉？”

===Original===
“When you came back upstairs from the kitchen, did you notice anything about the atmosphere of Mrs. Endicott’s room?”

“Why—I don’t know—you mean a sense of tension or something?”

“No, I don’t. I mean was it as warm as when you left it, or cooler, or what?”

“Yes, I do, too—it was cooler—*much*. Because I remember after I quieted Roberts I went over to one of the radiators to see if the heat was still turned on. I thought Roberts must have turned it off, although I couldn’t for the life of me see why. But the radiator was quite hot, so I realized it must have been just the change from the kitchen. It’s a hot kitchen.”

“That is probably just what it was. Would you send Roberts to me now, please?”

“I will, Lieutenant.”

“Thank you.”

Miss Vickers went out and closed the door.

Lieutenant Valcour then did a rather horrible thing. He went over to the bed and pulled down enough of the sheet so that Endicott’s face was exposed.

And then he sat down and waited for Roberts.

===Chinese===
“您从厨房再回到楼上来的时候，有没有注意到恩迪科特夫人房里的气氛有什么异样？”

“这——我说不准——您是指一种紧张感什么的？”

“不，不是。我的意思是，房里是否和您离开时一样暖，还是更凉了，还是怎样？”

“哦，对了——是更凉了——凉*得多*。因为我记得，我把罗伯茨安抚下来之后，还走到其中一片暖气片跟前，看了看是不是还在供暖。我以为是罗伯茨把它关掉了，尽管我怎么也想不通她为什么要这么做。可那暖气片烫得很，于是我明白过来，那无非是从厨房出来之后的反差。那可是间热厨房。”

“多半正是这么回事。那请您现在就把罗伯茨叫来，好吗？”

“好的，中尉。”

“多谢。”

维克斯小姐出去，带上了门。

瓦尔考中尉随后做了一件相当骇人的事。他走到床边，把床单往下拉了拉，一直拉到恩迪科特的脸整个露出来为止。

然后他坐下来，等着罗伯茨。

## XX 3:24 a.m.—On Private Heights / 二十 凌晨三时二十四分——在孤高的高处

===Original===
“You wanted to see me, Lieutenant?”

She *had* been under a strain, and a rather terrible one. There wasn’t any doubt about that. It was emotion, after all, that brought age, not years, thought Lieutenant Valcour as he glanced at the dark rings so clearly visible beneath her tragic eyes.

Roberts hadn’t looked toward the bed—yet—but then he hadn’t really expected that she would. Perhaps she wouldn’t look for some time, but eventually she would lose some portion of that really splendid self-control that she was exerting and then, instead of the expanse of white sheet she had been expecting, there would be Endicott’s face. …

===Chinese===
“您要见我，中尉？”

她*确实*一直处在一种重压之下，而且是相当可怕的重压。这一点毋庸置疑。终究催人老的是情绪，而非年岁——瓦尔考中尉暗想，一面瞥了一眼她那双悲戚的眼睛底下清晰可见的黑眼圈。

罗伯茨还没有朝那张床望过——眼下还没有——可他原本也没真指望她会望过去。说不定好一阵子她都不会去看，可她终究会有那么一刻，绷不住她此刻正极力维持的那份实在堪称精彩的自制的一角，到那时，映入她眼帘的就不再是她一直料想着的那片白床单，而将是恩迪科特的脸。……

===Original===
“I wonder if you could tell me, Miss Roberts, the number of shots that were fired during the shooting.”

“I’m sure I couldn’t.”

She was pointedly on guard, her eyes held at a level that included his cravat but went no higher.

“The question isn’t as silly a one as it seems,” Lieutenant Valcour said. “I don’t suggest for a minute that you counted the shots as they were being fired, actually, but it’s quite within possibility that your subconscious mind really did that very thing, and that on consciously thinking about it the number might come to you. It’s something along the principle of visualizing sound.”

“I’m sorry. I’m sure that no amount of thinking about it would clear the rather terrible confusion of that moment.”

“Won’t you sit down?”

“I prefer to stand, thank you.”

“Just as you wish. You were with Mrs. Endicott, weren’t you, when it happened?”

“Yes.”

Lieutenant Valcour admired the accomplished ease with which the word had so unhesitatingly been brought out; but then most women, in his estimation, were natural-born liars. The art formed for him one of their greatest charms.

“You were sitting down beside the bed?” he went on.

“Yes. Reading.”

Splendid—splendid—she was a Bernhardt—a Duse.

===Chinese===
“不知您能不能告诉我，罗伯茨小姐，开枪的时候一共响了几枪。”

“我肯定说不上来。”

她分明是存着戒备的，两眼凝定在某个高度上——那高度堪堪收进他的领结，却再不肯往上抬半分。

“这问题并不像它看上去那么蠢，”瓦尔考中尉说，“我丝毫不是在暗示您当时一枪一枪数了过去，可您的潜意识倒真有可能恰恰做了这件事，等到您有意识地琢磨起来，那个数字没准儿就会浮上心头。这里头的道理，跟把声音转化为画面那种本事是相通的。”

“对不起。我敢说，再怎么琢磨，也理不清那一刻那相当可怕的混乱。”

“您不坐下吗？”

“我宁愿站着，谢谢您。”

“随您的便。事情发生的时候，您正和恩迪科特夫人在一起，是吗？”

“是的。”

瓦尔考中尉钦佩这一个字吐出来时那毫不迟疑的、训练有素的从容；不过话说回来，在他看来，多数女人都是天生的说谎家。这门功夫于他，正是她们最大的魅力之一。

“您当时正坐在床边？”他接着问。

“是的。在看书。”

精彩——精彩——她是一个伯恩哈特（Bernhardt）——一个杜丝（Duse）。

===Original===
“And Miss Vickers?”

“She was down in the kitchen making some coffee.”

“Did the shooting upset you, Miss Roberts?”

“I’m naturally nervous. The sound of firing has always disturbed me terribly.” Then she flung at him abruptly, “My brother was killed in the war.”

Lieutenant Valcour both looked and felt genuinely consoling. He also felt a selfish measure of irritation. The statement was such a perfect period mark. When a young woman, no matter how great a criminal, potentially, announces flatly that her brother has been killed during the war, one can’t ride over the fact roughshod.

===Chinese===
“维克斯小姐（Miss Vickers）呢？”

“她在楼下厨房里煮咖啡。”

“开枪的事让您受惊了吗，罗伯茨小姐？”

“我天生就神经质。枪声一向叫我极其难受。”接着她蓦地冲他甩过来一句，“我哥哥是打仗时阵亡的。”

瓦尔考中尉无论神情还是心境，都当真是宽慰的。他也感到了一丝出于私心的恼火。这句话简直是一个再完美不过的句号。当一位年轻女子，无论她可能是个何等重大的罪犯，斩钉截铁地宣告她的哥哥是在战争中阵亡的时候，谁也没法若无其事地从这一事实上践踏过去。

===Original===
“Was there anyone whom you loved killed in the war, Lieutenant?”

She was determined to hammer at the point, it seemed. He wished that she would stop.

“There wasn’t, Miss Roberts.”

“Then you don’t know much about soldiers.”

“No, not much, really.”

“I don’t mean soldiers—or the war itself, either. It’s a state of being—a sort of lucid abnormality. It’s hard to tell you just what I do mean. But it’s the thing,” she ended fiercely, “that made me understand Mr. Endicott. He never quite recovered, you see, from being a soldier.”

“And perhaps it also made you understand why Mrs. Endicott misunderstood him?”

Things were going better now; the channel was broadening into useful seas.

“Of course it was,” Roberts said. “She, too, lost no one in the war.”

The fog rolled in again.

“I’m afraid I’m not following you very clearly.”

“It’s quite useless, Lieutenant—simply that in Mr. Endicott I kept seeing my brother. I suffered for him to the extent I would have suffered for my brother had my brother been in similar circumstances.”

===Chinese===
“您可有哪位亲爱的人是打仗时阵亡的吗，中尉？”

看来她是铁了心要在这桩事上反复纠缠。他真希望她能打住。

“没有，罗伯茨小姐。”

“那您对当兵的人就不大了解了。”

“是的，不大，确实如此。”

“我指的并不是当兵的人——也不是战争本身。那是一种存在状态——一种清明的反常。我很难跟您说清楚我到底是什么意思。可正是这个东西，”她激烈地收尾道，“让我能理解恩迪科特先生。您要知道，他自从当过兵之后，就再没能真正复原。”

“那么，或许也正是它，让您理解了恩迪科特夫人为什么会误解他？”

这会儿事情倒顺手起来了；航道正拓宽成一片可资利用的汪洋。

“当然是的，”罗伯茨说，“她也在战争中没失去过任何人。”

迷雾又涌了上来。

“恐怕我没太听明白您的话。”

“说了也是白搭，中尉——只不过是，在恩迪科特先生身上，我一再看见我哥哥。我为他受的苦，到了假如我哥哥身处相似境地我也会为他受的那般苦的地步。”

===Original===
“Suffered?”

“Yes, suffered. From her damned superiority.”

“You think that Mrs. Endicott overdid the mental?”

He noted that Roberts was slowly losing control. There was a blazing quality of anger creeping into her eyes.

“Lieutenant, she regarded that man as her tame tiger. You realize how strong he must have been physically.”

“Very strong.”

“It used to please her to control him—you know the way it’s commonly expressed—with a ‘word.’”

“I shouldn’t exactly say that she had succeeded.”

“The other women?”

“Yes.”

“She didn’t care about that. If anything, it satisfied her sense of power. She looked on them as a pack of shoddy substitutes that he could fool with, kick around, and treat terribly, if he liked. But she still remained the original—the unapproachable—the happy possessor of a tame tiger. He was always *hers*, you see, no matter what it was he had done. She’s had him crying.”

===Chinese===
“受苦？”

“是的，受苦。受她那该死的优越感的苦。”

“您觉得恩迪科特夫人在智识那一头做得太过分了？”

他留意到，罗伯茨正一点点地失去自制。一种灼热如火的怒意正悄悄爬进她的眼睛。

“中尉，她把那个男人当作她豢养的一只驯服的老虎。您可想而知，他的体魄该有多么强壮。”

“非常强壮。”

“从前取悦她的，是用一个‘字眼’便能辖制住他——您知道人们通常是怎么说法的——这么一种事。”

“我倒不大会说，她当真做到了。”

“那些别的女人？”

“是的。”

“她并不在乎那些。非要说不可，那反倒满足了她的权力欲。在她看来，她们不过是一群蹩脚的替代品，他尽可以拿她们寻开心，把她们踢来踢去，任意糟蹋，只要他乐意。可她仍旧是那原配——那无人可及的——那豢养着一只驯服老虎的幸福主人。您看，他始终是*她的*，无论他做过什么。她曾把他弄哭过。”

===Original===
“That’s a little hard to believe.”

“It’s the truth. He took her in his hands one night and twisted her—just like that! She didn’t say a thing to him. For a month afterward he went around the house like a whipped cat. Then she said something kind to him, and he cried. I wish she was in hell.”

“Perhaps she is, Miss Roberts—just that.”

“She won’t stay in it long. Her kind doesn’t.”

Lieutenant Valcour held his eyes thoughtfully directed toward the bed.

“Tell me, Miss Roberts, do you think that Mr. Endicott is happier dead? Let me put it in this fashion: if Mr. Endicott had really been your brother, would you rather have seen him dead than living in the emotional hell you picture Mr. Endicott as having lived in?”

His gaze retained its determined fixity.

“No,” she said. “There is always a way out.” It was irresistible. She found herself having to look, too. Against every advice of instinct her eyes were drawn toward the bed in company with Lieutenant Valcour’s … peace—there *was* peace—greater than she had ever seen when he had been living—peace to a tired heart—a plain, normal, happy human heart that had been broken on the wheel of too much complexity. … “Oh, I’m lying, Lieutenant! I would—I would—a million times rather.”

===Chinese===
“这未免有点难以置信。”

“这是实话。有一天夜里，他把她抓在手里，拧了她一把——就这么一下！她一句话也没对他说。之后足足一个月，他在屋子里走动，像一只挨了打的猫。后来她对他说了句什么体贴的话，他就哭了。我巴不得她下地狱。”

“说不定她已经在里头了，罗伯茨小姐——正就是那样。”

“她在里头待不长的。她那号人待不长。”

瓦尔考中尉若有所思地把目光定在那张床上。

“告诉我，罗伯茨小姐，您觉得恩迪科特先生死了反而更幸福吗？容我这样来问：倘若恩迪科特先生当真是您的哥哥，您是宁愿看见他死了，还是宁愿他活在您所描绘的、恩迪科特先生曾经活过的那种情感地狱里？”

他的目光保持着一种执拗的凝定。

“不，”她说，“总有出路可寻。”这是无法抗拒的。她发觉自己也不得不看过去。全然违逆着本能的每一道告诫，她的眼睛被牵引着，随瓦尔考中尉的目光一同转向那张床……安宁——那里面*确乎*有安宁——比他活着时她所见过的任何时刻都更深——一个疲惫之心的安宁——一颗朴实、正常、幸福的人心，被过于繁复的轮盘碾碎之后所得的安宁。……“哦，我在撒谎，中尉！我愿意——我愿意——千百万倍地宁愿那样。”

===Original===
He worked very fast now, having captured the mood. “Were you thinking of all that when you stood outside on the balcony and watched him through the window?”

Her eyes clung immovably to the cold closed lids, the mouth, carved in gentle shadows; her very being seemed withdrawn on private heights. “I wasn’t on the balcony.”

“And I’d like to know what you did with the gun.”

… Perhaps he was laughing at it all now, if people laugh in heaven. He and her brother. They would have met and be laughing at it all together. But they wouldn’t be laughing at her. … “There wasn’t any need to use the gun, Lieutenant.”

“Then what did you do with it?”

“Put it back in the bottom of my trunk.” … He’d know, now, the exact reason why she had done the things that she had done. People know everything in heaven—sort of an enveloping awareness—like lightning darting brilliantly to immediate comprehension at its target—target—gun?—*gun*. Her face was bleak ivory. “What did you say, Lieutenant?”

“I had just asked you, Miss Roberts, what you did with the gun, and you told me that you put it back again in the bottom of your trunk.”

Her eyes, as she looked at him, were strangely devoid of fear.

“Then if I told you that, you’ll find it there.”

“It wasn’t the wisest place to put it, Miss Roberts.”

“It doesn’t matter much.”

“You mean you don’t care?”

“Not just that. I’m speaking about the gun. I never fired it.”

===Chinese===
此刻他极快地乘胜追击，既已抓住了这一阵心绪。“您站在外头阳台上，隔着窗子望着他的时候，心里想的就是这一切吗？”

她的眼睛一动不动地凝在那两片冰凉合拢的眼睑上，凝在那张在柔和阴影中如雕刻出来的嘴上；她整个存在仿佛已退守到一片孤高的高处。“我没有站在阳台上。”

“而我倒想知道，您把那把枪怎么处置了。”

……说不定他这会儿正在天上嘲笑这一切呢，倘若人在天堂也会发笑的话。他，和她的哥哥。他们想必已经相会，正一道嘲笑着这全部的事。可他们不会嘲笑她。……“根本没必要用那把枪，中尉。”

“那您把它怎么处置了？”

“放回我那只大箱子的箱底了。”……这会儿他该知道了，她所做的那些事，究竟为着什么。人在天堂里是什么都知道的——那是一种无所不包的知觉——像一道闪电，炫目地直射向它所击中的目标，登时便尽皆了然——目标——枪？——*枪*。她的脸化作一片惨白的象牙。“您刚才说什么，中尉？”

“我刚才问您来着，罗伯茨小姐，您把那把枪怎么处置了，您告诉我，您把它又放回了您那只大箱子的箱底。”

她望着他时，那双眼睛竟奇异地毫无惧色。

“那么，要是我对您这么说了，您就会在那里头找到它。”

“那可不是个最聪明的藏枪之处，罗伯茨小姐。”

“这没多大要紧。”

“您的意思是您不在乎？”

“不全是那个意思。我说的是那把枪。我从没开过它。”

===Original===
“Then why did you hide it?”

“Because it’s illegal to have a gun.”

“Then why did you have one, Miss Roberts?”

“It’s one my brother gave me over twelve years ago. I’ve always kept it with me.”

“What calibre is it?”

“A Colt .38.”

The bullet in Lieutenant Valcour’s pocket had been fired from a Colt .38.

“And tonight you were going to use it to save Mr. Endicott by shooting him.”

“No, Lieutenant. I was going to use it to shoot Mrs. Endicott if she attempted to get near him again.”

“Again?”

“Why, yes, Lieutenant. She went out of the room last night right after he had knocked and said goodbye.”

“Out into the hallway?”

“Yes.”

“When did she come back?”

“She didn’t come back.”

“Then when was the next time you saw her?”

“When you rang for me—after you had found Mr. Endicott in the cupboard.”

“And you think it was Mrs. Endicott who put him there.” Lieutenant Valcour thought for a moment of the broken finger nail of Mrs. Endicott’s otherwise immaculate hand. “But why, Miss Roberts, should she kill her—tiger?”

“Perhaps Mr. Hollander could tell you that better than I.”

“And why did you get a gun to prevent Mrs. Endicott from going again to her husband, when you knew she was under the influence of a narcotic, that she was unconscious, and couldn’t possibly move?”

“Because, Lieutenant, she never drank the narcotic.”

===Chinese===
“那您为什么又要把它藏起来？”

“因为私藏枪支是违法的。”

“那您又为什么会有这么一把枪呢，罗伯茨小姐？”

“那是我哥哥十二年前给我的。我一直把它带在身边。”

“是什么口径？”

“一把柯尔特点三八（Colt .38）。”

瓦尔考中尉口袋里那颗子弹，正是从一把柯尔特点三八里射出来的。

“而今晚您本打算用它击毙恩迪科特先生，好以此救他一命。”

“不，中尉。我是打算用它来击毙恩迪科特夫人，假如她再试图接近他。”

“再？”

“是的呀，中尉。昨晚他来敲门道别之后，她立刻就走出了这个房间。”

“走到外面的过道里？”

“是的。”

“她什么时候回来的？”

“她没有回来。”

“那您下一次见到她是什么时候？”

“您按铃叫我的时候——在您于衣橱里发现恩迪科特先生之后。”

“这么说您认定，把他放进衣橱的是恩迪科特夫人。”瓦尔考中尉一时想起了恩迪科特夫人那只除了一处断裂的指甲之外无可挑剔的手。“可是，罗伯茨小姐，她为什么要杀她的——老虎呢？”

“这事霍兰德先生说不定能比我答得更清楚。”

“那您又为什么要去取一把枪，好阻止恩迪科特夫人再回到她丈夫身边呢，既然您明知她受了麻醉剂的作用，神志不清，根本动弹不得？”

“因为，中尉，她根本就没有喝下那麻醉剂。”

## XXI 3:51 a.m.—A Woman’s Slipper / 二十一 凌晨三时五十一分——一只女鞋

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour felt a distinct shock, and his eyes became predatorily alert. If this astonishing thing was true and Mrs. Endicott had not taken the narcotic prepared for her by Dr. Worth, then the bypaths one might dart along were numerous and alarming indeed.

“How do you know, Miss Roberts?” he said.

“Because when the nurse went downstairs to make that coffee I went over to the bed. I wanted to take a close look at Mrs. Endicott. Have you ever felt that desire to look closely at something that you hate very much? It’s the curiosity of hate, I suppose. I put my hand on the spread, at the edge, so that I could lean down. The spread was damp; something had been poured on it. There wasn’t anything that could have been poured on it except the narcotic. She’d recovered consciousness, you see, when the nurse and Dr. Worth brought her in from here and put her to bed.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉感到一阵明显的震动，眼睛变得如猛禽般警觉。倘若这桩惊人的事属实，恩迪科特夫人当真没有服下沃思医生为她调制的安眠药，那么人所能一闪身钻入的岔路，确乎又多又令人心惊。

“您怎么知道的，罗伯茨小姐？”他问。

“因为护士下楼去煮咖啡的时候，我走到了床边。我想凑近仔仔细细瞧一瞧恩迪科特夫人。你可曾体会过那种冲动——想紧盯着一样你深恶痛绝的东西看个仔细？我想，那是仇恨所生的好奇心。我把一只手按在床罩上，按在边上，好让我俯下身去。那床罩是潮的；有什么东西泼在了上面。除了那安眠药，再没有什么东西可能被泼在上面了。您要知道，护士和沃思医生把她从这里扶出去、安顿到床上的时候，她已经醒了过来。”

===Original===
“But wouldn’t he or the nurse have seen her pour it out?”

“None of us saw it, Lieutenant, because she said, just after the doctor had handed her the glass, ‘There’s blood on that dresser.’ We all looked at the dresser, of course. Naturally there wasn’t any blood on it. The doctor thought she was delirious. She was just finishing drinking when we turned around.”

“Didn’t you accuse her—when you felt the damp spot on the spread?”

===Chinese===
“可他或者护士难道没瞧见她把药倒掉吗？”

“我们谁也没瞧见，中尉，因为医生把玻璃杯递给她之后，她便说：‘那梳妆台上有血。’我们当然都去看梳妆台了。上面自然没有什么血。医生以为她在说胡话。等我们转过身来，她已把药喝完了。”

“您摸到床罩上那块潮湿的地方时——难道没有当面质问她？”

===Original===
“What was the use? She never would have admitted it. I believe,” Roberts said fiercely, “that I could have stuck pins in her and that she’d have endured the pain rather than admit it. And suddenly I began to feel afraid—not so much of her, as of what she might do to Mr. Endicott. She was playing a trick and I didn’t know just what the purpose of it was. I ran upstairs and got my gun, then came right back.”

“She was still in bed?”

“Yes. But the shooting was over, and the room was cold. The room was cold”—Roberts’s voice was very intense as she drove her points home—“and her skin was cold, and her breathing was heavy from recent exertion. I think I was going to kill her. I would have killed her if the nurse hadn’t come in just then.”

===Chinese===
“有什么用？她绝不会承认的。我相信，”罗伯茨咬牙切齿地说，“我就是拿针去扎她，她也宁可忍着疼，断不肯认账。猛然间我开始觉得害怕——倒不全是怕她，而是怕她会对恩迪科特先生下什么手。她在耍一个花招，而我偏偏摸不清这花招究竟为着什么。我跑上楼去取了我的枪，紧接着便赶了回来。”

“她还在床上？”

“是的。可枪声已经响过了，屋里是凉的。屋里是凉的”——罗伯茨的声音极其急切，一句句把话钉死——“她的皮肤也是凉的，她的呼吸因为方才那一番使劲而沉重。我想，我是要杀她的。要不是护士恰在那时走了进来，我准会把她杀了。”

===Original===
“Why didn’t you tell someone of this at once, Miss Roberts?”

“Would you have? Would anyone have?”

“I don’t quite understand.”

“There had just been that shooting—and I had a gun. I wanted to get rid of it. By the time I had got rid of it, it was too late. I couldn’t say anything then without practically accusing myself of a murder I didn’t commit.”

“You’ll stay here in the house, Miss Roberts?”

===Chinese===
“您为什么没有立刻把这事告诉谁呢，罗伯茨小姐？”

“换了您会吗？换了谁会吗？”

“我不大明白。”

“刚刚才响过那一枪——而我手里有枪。我只想把它处理掉。等我处理完了，已经太迟了。那时我再开口，无异于把自己指控为一桩并非我所为的谋杀案里的凶手。”

“您会留在这所宅子里吧，罗伯茨小姐？”

===Original===
“Naturally, since I’m to be accused of having killed Mr. Endicott.”

“Not as yet, Miss Roberts.”

“It won’t bother me.” She added bitterly, as she started for the door, “You’ll find me a tractable prisoner.”

“One minute please, Miss Roberts. How long were you gone from Mrs. Endicott’s room when you went upstairs to get the gun?”

===Chinese===
“那自然，既然人家要说我杀了恩迪科特先生。”

“眼下还不至于，罗伯茨小姐。”

“那也碍不着我。”她向门口走去时，苦涩地补了一句，“你们会发觉我是个听话的囚徒。”

“请稍等片刻，罗伯茨小姐。您上楼去取枪、离开恩迪科特夫人房间的这段时间，有多长？”

===Original===
“Just long enough to run up and back again. I have no idea, really.”

“Where is your room?”

“On the upper floor—the room to the left of the corridor in the front of the house.”

“And whereabouts did you keep the gun?”

“In my trunk—where it is now.”

“Was the trunk locked?”

===Chinese===
“也就够我跑上去再跑回来的工夫。说真的，我说不上来。”

“您的房间在哪儿？”

“在上一层——房子前头走廊靠左的那间。”

“那您把枪收在哪儿？”

“在我的箱子里——就是它眼下待的地方。”

“箱子上锁了吗？”

===Original===
“Yes. I keep it locked.”

“And the keys for it?”

“In a purse. The purse was in a dresser drawer.”

“Then that gives us a pretty good idea of the length of time you must have been gone, doesn’t it?”

“I suppose it does. Three or four minutes, probably.”

“Nearer, I imagine, to five or six. But we don’t require the actual number of minutes. The point we need is, rather, a comparison of two different operations within the same time limit. While you were going through the various movements you have described, would Mrs. Endicott have had the time to get out of bed, supply herself with a revolver, open a window, and, from the balcony, shoot Mr. Endicott, return to her room, and be in bed again by the time you came down? I think so, don’t you?”

===Chinese===
“锁了。我一直锁着它。”

“钥匙呢？”

“在一只手提包里。手提包放在梳妆台的抽屉里。”

“那么您离开的那段时间有多长，我们心里就颇有数了，不是吗？”

“我想是吧。大概三四分钟。”

“我琢磨，只怕更接近五六分钟。不过我们所需要的，并非确切的分钟数。我们所需要的，毋宁说是在同一段时间之内，就两套不同的动作作一番比较。当您在完成您所描述的那一连串举动时，恩迪科特夫人可有工夫下床、为自己弄到一把左轮手枪、推开一扇窗、从阳台上朝恩迪科特先生开枪，再回到自己房里，并在您下来之前重新躺回床上？我想是有的，您说呢？”

===Original===
“There would have been plenty of time for that.”

“You’ve been with Mrs. Endicott for quite a while. Have you ever noticed whether or not she owns a pistol?”

“I don’t think I have. No, I’m sure I’ve never seen one. That doesn’t prove anything, though. There are any number of private places where she may have kept it. It is also possible”—Roberts seemed desperately earnest in her effort to strengthen each link in her accusation, for she was accusing rather than simply offering a theory—“that someone may recently have given her a revolver, isn’t it?”

“Everything is possible.”

“Mr. Hollander, for example?”

“A very good example.”

===Chinese===
“做这些事的工夫，是绰绰有余的。”

“您跟着恩迪科特夫人有些日子了。您可曾留意过她有没有手枪？”

“我想我没留意过。不，我肯定从没见过一把。可这也说明不了什么。她可能藏枪的隐秘去处，多得数不胜数。也还有这样的可能”——罗伯茨像是在拼命较着劲儿，要把她这条指控的每一环都拧得死死的，因为她这是在指控，而非仅仅摆出一个推测——“近来某人送了她一把左轮手枪，这也有可能，不是吗？”

“什么事都是可能的。”

“比方说——霍兰德（Hollander）先生？”

“一个极恰当的例子。”

===Original===
He said nothing further, and after a while the stillness became almost physically oppressive. Roberts was finished with emotions. “Is that all?” she said, and her voice was colourless.

“I believe so, Miss Roberts—except that I wish you would tell me why, in view of your recent insinuations concerning Mrs. Endicott and Hollander, you ever suggested him as the proper friend to stay with her husband tonight. It’s a little inconsistent, don’t you think?”

“Very.”

“Then why did you do it?”

“I have nothing further to say.”

===Chinese===
他没有再说什么，过了一会儿，那寂静几乎变成了一种带着实体般的压迫感。罗伯茨的情绪已经耗尽了。“就这些了吗？”她说，声音没有一丝色彩。

“我想就这些了，罗伯茨小姐——只不过我希望您能告诉我，既然您方才对恩迪科特夫人和霍兰德之间作了那么多含沙射影，您当初又怎会提议让他今晚留下来陪伴她丈夫。这未免有些自相矛盾，您不觉得吗？”

“是很矛盾。”

“那您又为何要那样提议？”

“我再没什么要说的了。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour went abruptly to the door and opened it. Cassidy and Hansen were standing near by in the corridor.

“Hansen,” he said, “go with Miss Roberts up to her room. There is a gun in her trunk. She will give it to you. Keep it for me.”

“Yes, sir.”

Roberts went outside.

“Am I to consider myself under arrest, Lieutenant?”

“No, Miss Roberts. But, as I have explained, you are not to leave the house. Cassidy, come inside here with me.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉蓦地走到门口，把门拉开。卡西迪（Cassidy）和汉森（Hansen）正站在走廊里离门不远的地方。

“汉森，”他说，“陪罗伯茨小姐上楼到她房里去。她箱子里有一把枪。她会交给您。替我收好。”

“是，长官。”

罗伯茨走了出去。

“我是不是该把自己当作已被捕了，中尉？”

“还没有，罗伯茨小姐。不过正如我已经说明的，您不得离开这所宅子。卡西迪，跟我进来。”

===Original===
Cassidy came in and closed the door. He watched Lieutenant Valcour draw the sheet up again over Endicott’s face.

“What’s Dr. Worth doing, Cassidy?”

“He has gone back to bed, sir. Shall I go get him?” Cassidy cast one suspicious look toward the bed.

“No, let him sleep. There’s nothing just this instant. I’ll want to see him in about a quarter of an hour, though.”

===Chinese===
卡西迪走进来，带上了门。他看着瓦尔考中尉重新把床单拉起，盖回恩迪科特的脸上。

“沃思医生在做什么，卡西迪？”

“他已经回床上去睡了，长官。要我去把他叫起来吗？”卡西迪朝那张床投去一道满含疑虑的目光。

“不必，让他睡吧。眼下这一刻没什么事。不过，大约一刻钟后我要见他。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour went into the bathroom, opened the window, and went outside onto the balcony. The gray before dawning was in the sky, and a rare clearness was vibrant in the fresh, sweet air.

The outline of the garden down below was quite distinct. There were other gardens belonging to the adjacent houses, too, and to the houses backing them from the rear. It was a street of gardens which bloomed, Lieutenant Valcour reflected, for the express benefit of caretakers in summer, while their owners spent the season at fashionable resorts either in the mountains or on the shore.

Lieutenant Valcour went and carefully examined with his flashlight the window to Endicott’s room that had been raised from the bottom when the shot was fired. He played the light upon the surface of its glass. It was quite clean. There was no trace of any pressing of noses or of foreheads against its polished surface. Nor, on the stone sill, were there any telltale threads of silk, or any of the various clues that would serve to indicate a woman’s presence.

He stared speculatively for a minute at the windows of the room above, where the curiously vindictive Mrs. Siddons was now presumably resting, or else indulging in her blank-eyed game of mental maledictions. No, he couldn’t really visualize her as descending to the balcony by a rope or any other kind of ladder. A hundred years ago, perhaps, she might have gone so far as to shape a replica of Mr. Endicott in wax and then, with appropriate incantations, proceed to stick pins in such portions of it as would cabalistically do the most good. But there was no such simple expedient left her in our modern skeptic age. It would be necessary, of course, to interview her further concerning those vague, bitter hints she had thrown out about outrageous actions on the part of Endicott toward the maids.

Even the city could not kill the fair fresh breezes of dawn. He stared at the dimming stars and wondered whether Roberts’s extraordinary statement was a lie. For after all it hinged upon nothing more significant than a damp spot at the edge of a spread, and Roberts could easily have spilled something there herself to offer as corroborative evidence to her tale. Was she, he wondered, quite so smart? And from all that he had been able to judge of her, he rather thought that she was.

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉走进浴室，推开窗户，出到外面的阳台上。天际已泛起破晓前那种灰白，一种难得的澄澈在清新甜美的空气里跃动着。

下面那座花园的轮廓相当分明。此外还有别的花园，分属两旁相邻的宅子，以及从背后与之相背的房子。这是一条花园夹道的街，瓦尔考中尉暗想，这些花园到了夏天只为看守人们而盛开，而它们的主人都到山间或海滨那些时髦的避暑胜地消夏去了。

瓦尔考中尉走过去，借手电筒仔细查验枪响时从底部推起的那扇恩迪科特房间的窗户。他把光投在玻璃面上。玻璃上相当干净。上面寻不见任何鼻子或额头抵压在那光洁表面上的痕迹。那石头的窗台上，也找不出一根足以泄密的丝线，或任何能表明有女人曾到过此处的蛛丝马迹。

他若有所思地朝楼上那间房的窗户端详了一阵——那位怨毒得出奇的西登斯夫人（Mrs. Siddons），此刻想必正在里面歇息，要不然就是在尽情玩着她那种眼神空洞的、在心头诅咒人的把戏。不，他实在没法想象她会顺着绳索、或别的什么梯子下到这阳台上。换作一百年前，她或许真会做出那种事来——用蜡塑一个恩迪科特先生的替身，再念着相称的咒语，往那蜡人身上几处按神秘学的说法最能奏效的部位扎针。可到了我们这个怀疑一切的现代，那样简便的法子她已然用不成了。当然，关于她先前含糊而尖刻地暗示过的、恩迪科特对女佣们所行的那些不堪之事，他还得再找她谈谈。

即便是这城市，也扼杀不了黎明时分那些明媚清新的微风。他凝望渐次暗淡的星光，心里暗暗琢磨罗伯茨那番离奇的话会不会是谎言。说到底，它所凭依的，不过是床罩边沿上那一块潮湿罢了——而罗伯茨大可以自己在那里泼上点什么，好给自己的说辞添一份佐证。她究竟有没有那么精明呢——他暗想。而就他到目前为止对她所能作出的判断而言，他倒觉得她确有那般精明。

===Original===
He would have to consult with Dr. Worth, of course, before doing anything drastic. And the doctor would probably raise a holler, especially since he had just gone to bed and would have to be yanked summarily out of it again. Well, bed-yankings were to be expected in the lives of doctors and of the police; they were expected to be perpetually on tap, like heat or water.

He made his way slowly toward the windows of Mrs. Endicott’s room, carefully inspecting the balcony and sills with his flashlight as he went along. There were no smudges, no threads, no clues until he reached the last window in the row. And there, on the balcony floor just below its sash, something blazed in the circle of his torch a bright jade green.

It was a woman’s slipper.

===Chinese===
当然，在采取任何果决的行动之前，他还得先同沃思医生商量。而医生多半要大叫大嚷一番的，尤其他刚刚才回到床上，转眼又要被人一把薅起来。唉，这种被人从床上薅起来的事，在医生和警察的生活里原是意料之中；他们都得永远听候差遣，就跟暖气和水龙头一个样。

他慢慢朝恩迪科特夫人房间的几扇窗户走去，一路借手电筒细细查验着阳台和窗台。没有污痕，没有丝线，没有任何线索，直到他来到这一排的最后一扇窗前。就在那里，在那扇窗的窗框下方的阳台地面上，手电筒的光圈里，有一样东西灼灼闪耀着明亮的翠绿。

那是一只女鞋。

## XXII 4:14 a.m.—Tap—Tap—Tap / 二十二 凌晨四时十四分——哒——哒——哒

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour picked the slipper up and sighed. It was a distressingly leading and decisive clue, but it did not lead in a direction he cared to follow, nor did it decide things as he thought they ought to be decided.

On the surface of it, the case seemed blatantly plain: Hollander had come to the house at seven to save Mrs. Endicott from committing murder or suicide and had shocked Endicott almost to death—and just a short while ago Mrs. Endicott had shot her husband to prevent him from making a statement that would convict Hollander.

Rubbish!

Lieutenant Valcour flatly refused to believe it. And yet one had to believe that Hollander had certainly intended to stab Endicott with that knife; the point was irrefutable. Furthermore, Hollander’s motives remained clear enough and beautifully simple: he wanted to protect Mrs. Endicott.

But what about her motives?

And Roberts’s?

And as a kernel to the whole perplexing enigma, what had been the object of the search through Endicott’s pockets and among the papers in the left-hand upper drawer of his desk?

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉把那只鞋捡了起来，叹了一口气。这是一条恼人地直指方向、又铁板钉钉的线索，可它所指的方向他并不情愿循之而去，它所断定的结局也不是他所认为理当如此的那一种。

表面上看，这桩案子简直明摆到了露骨的地步：霍兰德七点钟来到这宅子，为的是把恩迪科特夫人从杀人或自杀的边缘救下来，结果几乎把恩迪科特吓得半死——而就在不久之前，恩迪科特夫人又开枪打死了自己的丈夫，为的是阻止他说出会让霍兰德定罪的话。

胡说八道！

瓦尔考中尉断然不肯相信。可又不得不信，霍兰德当初确是存心要用那把刀刺杀恩迪科特的；这一点无可辩驳。再者，霍兰德的动机依然够分明、够漂亮地简单：他要护住恩迪科特夫人。

可她的动机呢？

还有罗伯茨的呢？

而作为这整个扑朔迷离之谜的核心，当初翻遍恩迪科特口袋、又翻遍他书桌左上方抽屉里那些文件，所要找的究竟是什么？

===Original===
There was nothing to be gained, however, by standing outside on the balcony and admiring the flushing sky and breathing in with the manner of a connoisseur the morning air. Lieutenant Valcour returned, via the bathroom window, to Endicott’s room.

“The night’s almost over, Lieutenant,” said Cassidy by way of greeting.

“Almost over, Cassidy.”

“And it’s been a hell of a night, too, if you don’t mind my saying it.”

“I don’t mind your saying it.”

“Especially for him.”

Cassidy jerked a muscular thumb toward the bed.

“Least of all for him, Cassidy.”

“He may be well out of it at that.”

“He is. There’s a lot of beautiful tripe written about how all people kill the things they love. Metaphysically, perhaps. But with a bullet, Cassidy? Not so.”

“I don’t get you, Lieutenant.”

“That isn’t strange, Cassidy. So far I don’t even get myself.”

===Chinese===
可站在外头阳台上，观赏那渐渐泛红的天色、又装出一副行家的派头去嗅那清晨的空气，终究于事无补。瓦尔考中尉从浴室那扇窗子折回了恩迪科特的房间。

“这一夜总算快到头了，中尉。”卡西迪跟他打着招呼。

“快到头了，卡西迪。”

“再说这一夜也真是糟透了，我说这话您不见怪吧。”

“我不见怪。”

“尤其对他而言。”

卡西迪把那只筋肉虬结的大拇指朝那张床猛地一挑。

“最尤其的就是他，卡西迪。”

“这么一来，说不定他倒是解脱了。”

“他是解脱了。书上写了不知多少漂亮话，说人总会把他所爱的东西杀掉。玄学上也许是这样。可要换成一颗子弹呢，卡西迪？就并非如此了。”

“我没听懂您的意思，中尉。”

“这不奇怪，卡西迪。到这会儿为止，连我自己都还没弄明白我自己呢。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour went to the door and opened it. Hansen was standing outside, and in his hand was a gun wrapped in a clean handkerchief.

“Roberts’s gun, Hansen?”

“Yes, Lieutenant. It was just where you said it would be, in the trunk. I wrapped it in a handkerchief to keep any prints you might want on it.”

“That’s right, Hansen. Go upstairs now and wake up Dr. Worth. Ask him if he will please come down here at once.”

“Yes, Lieutenant.” Hansen hesitated for a minute.

“Well, what is it, Hansen?”

“I understood you all right didn’t I, sir,” Hansen said uncomfortably, “when you told me that maid wasn’t to be put under arrest?”

“Yes. I don’t want to do anything about her as yet. Later on we may book her on a violation of the Sullivan Law and again we may not.”

“Yes, sir.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉走到门前，把门打开。汉森正站在外头，手里是一把裹在干净手帕里的枪。

“是罗伯茨那把枪吗，汉森？”

“是的，中尉。就在您说的那个地方，在箱子里。我拿手帕把它包了起来，免得弄花了您说不定想要的指纹。”

“做得对，汉森。现在上楼去把沃思医生叫醒。问问他能不能劳驾马上下来一趟。”

“是，中尉。”汉森迟疑了一下。

“怎么了，汉森？”

“我刚才没听错吧，长官，”汉森不自在地说，“您吩咐过，说那个女佣不要抓起来，是这意思吧？”

“对。眼下我还不想动她。往后嘛，我们也许会以违反《沙利文法》的罪名传她，也许不会。”

“是，长官。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour took the gun and went back into the room with it, closing the door. He carefully unfolded enough of the handkerchief so that the barrel was exposed. He sniffed this and decided that the gun had neither been recently fired nor cleaned. There was just the definite odourlessness which one finds with guns that have not been used or taken care of for a very long time. So far, then, he was inclined to believe that Roberts’s story was correct.

“Is that the rod that done the trick, Lieutenant?” said Cassidy, who had been keenly interested in the sniffings.

“No, it isn’t, Cassidy. This gun hasn’t been fired for years, maybe.”

“Well, I wish it was. I’d like to get out of this joint.”

“Still nervous, Cassidy?”

“No, I ain’t nervous, Lieutenant. I’m just uncomfortable. It’s like there was something in this case that hasn’t broken yet. You know what I mean? Something we ain’t so much as put a finger on.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉接过枪，拿着它回到房里，把门关上。他小心翼翼地把手帕揭开足够的一角，露出枪管。他凑上去嗅了嗅，断定这把枪既未在近期开过火，也未擦拭过。它带着的，正是那种长久未曾使用、也无人保养的枪支才有的、确凿无误的无嗅无味。那么，到眼下为止，他倾向于相信罗伯茨说的是实话。

“干这件事的，就是这根铁家伙吗，中尉？”方才一直津津有味地盯着这番嗅探的卡西迪开口道。

“不是，卡西迪。这把枪兴许都好几年没开过了。”

“唉，我倒真希望是它。那我就好离开这鬼地方了。”

“还紧张吗，卡西迪？”

“不，我不紧张，中尉。我只是浑身不自在。这案子好像有样东西到现在还没露头。您明白我的意思吧？有样东西我们连手指头都还没碰到过。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour knew very well just exactly what Cassidy meant. He, too, felt that same indefinable effect of impending “somethings” that were connected with obscure danger. It was an emotion, however, which required official scowlings. After all, psychic patrolmen were not considered as being to the best interests of the force. One shouldn’t be allowed, really, to graduate into psychic realms anywhere below the rank of lieutenant.

“Discounting your weekly adventures between paper covers, this is your first real murder case, isn’t it, Cassidy?”

“I thank God it is, sir.”

“Well, you’ll get used to them after a while. Before you’re called in on your fourth or fifth you’ll be finished with having presentiments.”

“Will they be likely to be like this one, sir?”

“That will depend entirely, Cassidy, upon just how much publicity this one is given in the papers, as well as on the supply at hand of potential victims who have weak hearts. I dare say the method will become fashionable for a while.” There was a peevish rap on the door. “Ah, come in, Doctor.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉心里再清楚不过卡西迪指的是什么。他同样感觉得到，那种说不清道不明的、某种与隐晦危险相连的“东西”正在逼近的预感。然而这是一种需要用长官的蹙眉来加以压制的情绪。毕竟，通灵的巡警并不被看作警队的福音。说真的，在中尉这一级以下，谁也不该获准踏进通灵的境界。

“撇开你每周在封皮底下经历的那些历险不谈，这还是你头一桩货真价实的命案吧，卡西迪？”

“感谢上帝，是头一桩，长官。”

“嗯，过一阵你总会习惯的。等你被叫来办到第四桩、第五桩的时候，也就不会再有什么预感了。”

“那些案子，也多半会像这桩一样吗，长官？”

“那可就全看，卡西迪，报纸对这桩案子渲染到什么程度了，另外还得看手头有没有现成的、心脏不结实的那种潜在受害人。我敢说，这种手法兴许还会时兴上一阵。”这时，门上传来一阵带怒气的叩击声。“啊，请进，医生。”

===Original===
Dr. Worth was just as peevish as his knock. The camel’s-hair dressing gown in which he was still bundled hinted blurrily at indignant muscles that quivered beneath its loose folds. His hair was rumpled-looking and frowsy.

“Really, Lieutenant,” he began, “this is getting to be beyond a joke.”

“I’m sorry, Doctor, but I had to discuss Mrs. Endicott’s condition with you most seriously and at once.”

Dr. Worth paled a little at this.

“Nothing’s happened to her, too, has there?”

“No, Doctor, nothing has. And I don’t think that just now I could stand another murder. It’s about her physical condition in general. Is her heart all right?”

Dr. Worth’s curiosity was beginning to get the upper hand over his grouch.

“Perfectly sound. Why do you ask?”

“Because I want to try an experiment on her.”

“You want to what, sir?” Dr. Worth almost shouted it. He was thoroughly awake now.

“Not so loud, please, Doctor. I want you to let me stay in the room alone with your patient. You can open the connecting bathroom door a little and watch me through its crack, but I want the nurse out of the way. And I don’t want you to make any noise or comments while you’re watching. I don’t want Mrs. Endicott to know that you’re there.”

===Chinese===
沃思医生和他那阵敲门声一样带怒。他身上仍裹着那件驼绒晨衣，宽松的衣褶底下隐约透出愤愤不平、一阵阵打颤的肌肉。他的头发乱蓬蓬的，一副邋遢相。

“说真的，中尉，”他一开口就来，“这事儿可不能再当玩笑了。”

“对不住，医生，可我不得不就恩迪科特夫人的状况，立刻、极其郑重地跟您谈一谈。”

一听这话，沃思医生的脸色白了几分。

“她没也出什么事吧？”

“没有，医生，没有出。而且我想，眼下我这边再也经不住又一桩命案了。我想问的是她整体的身体状况。她的心脏好吗？”

沃思医生的好奇心已开始压倒他的那股别扭劲儿了。

“好得很。您怎么想起问这个？”

“因为我想在她身上试一试一个法子。”

“您要试什么，先生？”沃思医生几乎是嚷出来的。这会儿他已经彻底清醒了。

“请别这么大声，医生。我想请您让我一个人同您的病人待在那间屋里。您可以把相连的浴室门稍稍推开一点，从门缝里看着我，但我要那护士离开。而且您看着的时候，不要弄出一点声响，也不要作任何评说。我不想让恩迪科特夫人知道您在那里。”

===Original===
Dr. Worth looked at Lieutenant Valcour sharply. “This is nonsense. She couldn’t possibly tell who was or who wasn’t there. She’s unconscious.”

“Perhaps she isn’t, Doctor. This is what her maid has just told me.” Lieutenant Valcour offered Dr. Worth Roberts’s astonishing theory concerning the poured-out narcotic, and Dr. Worth was quite properly astonished. “So you see it’s a possibility, Doctor, and the fact of my finding that slipper outside of the window makes it practically a certainty.”

“It’s the most astounding thing I’ve ever heard of in my life. If you don’t intend to shock her, Lieutenant, I’ll agree to anything you say.”

“I shan’t do anything rough, Doctor, like discharging a gun off near her ear, or pinching her, or slapping her, or any of the tricks which are so popularly supposed to be kept up the sleeve of a policeman. You can stop me at any minute if you object to anything I may be doing.”

“Have you planned just what you will do?”

“With a woman like Mrs. Endicott there wouldn’t be any use in planning anything. All that I can do in advance is to create an atmosphere and then do whatever occurs to me as being best when the proper time comes. There won’t be anything complicated about it.”

“Just what sort of an atmosphere, Lieutenant?”

===Chinese===
沃思医生凌厉地看了瓦尔考中尉一眼。“这是无稽之谈。她根本不可能知道谁在、谁不在那里。她是不省人事的。”

“也许她并非如此，医生。这是她家的女佣方才告诉我的。”瓦尔考中尉把罗伯茨那套关于那剂被倒掉的麻醉药的惊人说法讲给沃思医生听，沃思医生理所当然地大为骇异。“所以您瞧，医生，这是有可能的，而我在窗外找到那只鞋这一桩事实，则几乎把它坐实了。”

“这是我这辈子听过的最骇人听闻的事。只要您不打算惊吓到她，中尉，您说什么我都依。”

“我不会动粗的，医生，比如在她耳边放一枪啦，捏她啦，掴她啦，或是任何人们普遍以为是藏在警察袖子里的那些花招。您要是看不惯我做的哪一桩，随时都能叫停。”

“您打算怎么做，可都盘算好了吗？”

“对付恩迪科特夫人这种女人，预先盘算什么都没有用。我所能事先做的，无非是布下一个气氛，到了合适的时候，再凭着当下冒到心头的、最妥当的法子去做。这里头没有什么复杂的名堂。”

“究竟是哪一种气氛呢，中尉？”

===Original===
“Well, in the first place I’ll call the nurse outside into the corridor and you can tell her not to go back in again until I say so. You might suggest to her that she go down to the kitchen and make some coffee—she seems a little dippy about coffee—or something. Then we’ll leave Mrs. Endicott quite alone in her room for a minute or two. If she’s really faking, she’ll begin to worry about what is going on. Then the door will open again and, instead of the nurse, I’ll come in. She’ll be pretty certain to suspect that I’ve found the slipper, but will be all the more careful to keep up her pretence of being under the influence of the narcotic. If she gets away with that, you know, she can always claim that Roberts herself must have dropped the slipper onto the balcony as a plant. The main thing is that Mrs. Endicott won’t know just what’s up, and when a woman of her temperament can’t figure a thing out mentally, it about drives her crazy.”

“Then I suppose, Lieutenant, that when you get her into this receptive state you’ll speak to her?”

Lieutenant Valcour laughed. “On the contrary, Doctor, I haven’t the slightest intention of saying a single word. Shall we go now? After you’ve arranged things with Nurse Vickers you can come back in here again and start watching from the bathroom.”

===Chinese===
“嗯，头一桩，我先把护士叫到外头的走廊上，您就吩咐她，没有我的话不要再进去。您不妨给她提个醒，让她到楼下厨房去煮点咖啡——她似乎对咖啡有点上瘾——或是别的什么。然后，我们就让恩迪科特夫人独自待在她房里一两分钟。她要果真是在装，就会开始犯起嘀咕：外头到底在搞什么名堂。接着，门会再次打开，进来的不是护士，而是我。她几乎一定会疑心我已经找到了那只鞋，可也正因如此，她会越发小心翼翼地维持着她受麻醉药所制的假象。您要知道，这一着她要是糊弄得过去，她尽可一口咬定：那只鞋必是罗伯茨自己丢到阳台上去、用来栽赃的。要紧的是，恩迪科特夫人将无从知道究竟出了什么事；而像她这种性子的女人，一旦在脑子里想不通一桩事，那简直要把她逼疯。”

“那么我想，中尉，等您把她引到这种入彀的状态，您就会同她说话了？”

瓦尔考中尉笑了起来。“恰恰相反，医生，我一丝一毫都不打算开口。我们现在就过去吗？等您把维克斯小姐那边安排妥当，就可以再回到这间房里，从浴室开始观看了。”

===Original===
They went outside, and Lieutenant Valcour rapped softly on Mrs. Endicott’s door. It opened a bit, and Nurse Vickers looked out. She saw Dr. Worth and came outside, shutting the door behind her.

“You wanted to see me, Doctor?”

“Yes, Miss Vickers. How is Mrs. Endicott?”

“Quite comfortable, Doctor. She’s breathing as peacefully as a child.”

“There haven’t been any signs of restlessness?”

“Oh, no, Doctor. She hasn’t budged since I’ve been watching her.”

Dr. Worth mildly raised his eyebrows. “That in itself is rather curious,” he said.

“Curious, Doctor?”

“Oh, nothing to be alarmed at, Miss Vickers. You look a little tired. Run downstairs and drink some coffee. The lieutenant, here, will stay with Mrs. Endicott, and you’re not to go back into her room again until he says so.”

“Help!” thought Lieutenant Valcour. As a detective Dr. Worth was a darned fine doctor. Miss Vickers, as he had expected, was instantly curious.

“Something more wrong, Doctor?”

“No Miss Vickers,” Lieutenant Valcour said coldly. “Please do as the doctor instructed, and at once.”

“Oh.”

Nurse Vickers, feeling a little outraged, vanished toward the stairs.

===Chinese===
两人走到外头，瓦尔考中尉在恩迪科特夫人的门上轻轻叩了叩。门开了一条缝，维克斯小姐从里面探出头来。她看见沃思医生，便走到外头，随手把门带上。

“您要见我，医生？”

“是的，维克斯小姐。恩迪科特夫人怎么样？”

“很安稳，医生。她呼吸得就像个孩子一样平静。”

“没有露出什么烦躁的迹象吧？”

“哦，没有，医生。我守在这儿起，她动都没动过一下。”

沃思医生轻轻地扬了扬眉。“这本身就颇有些古怪。”他说。

“古怪，医生？”

“哦，没什么可大惊小怪的，维克斯小姐。您看上去有点疲乏。到楼下去喝点咖啡吧。这位中尉会在这儿陪着恩迪科特夫人，您在他发话之前，不要再进她的房间了。”

“老天！”瓦尔考中尉暗想。沃思医生要当侦探的话，倒是一位再好不过的医生。不出他所料，维克斯小姐顿时起了好奇。

“又出了什么事吗，医生？”

“没有，维克斯小姐，”瓦尔考中尉冷冷地说，“请按医生的吩咐去做，马上去。”

“哦。”

维克斯小姐心里多少有点被冒犯，朝楼梯那边消失了。

===Original===
“Shall I go and stand by the bathroom door now?” said Dr. Worth.

“If you wish. Don’t make the slightest sound when you’re opening it, and don’t open it more than an inch at the most, please.”

“I won’t, Lieutenant.”

Dr. Worth, feeling very much like one of those fabulous characters he had read about in Fenimore Cooper when a child, went back into Endicott’s room.

Lieutenant Valcour waited another full minute before he opened the door and went inside. He did not look at Mrs. Endicott, but walked softly over to a chair, lifted it, and placed it close beside the bed. He drew the slipper from his pocket and sat down.

There was an utter and complete hush. For three minutes—he timed himself with his wrist watch—he sat motionless and stared at the closed lids of Mrs. Endicott’s eyes.

Then he began to tap the slipper quite softly, but quite persistently and with a rhythmic regularity, upon an arm of the chair.

Tap—tap—tap—tap—tap—

Mrs. Endicott’s face retained the smooth expressionlessness of slumber.

Tap—tap—tap—

Her breathing held the steady depths of sleep.

Tap—tap—tap—tap—

“If you do that much longer,” she said quietly, “I shall go insane.”

===Chinese===
“我现在就去守在浴室门边吗？”沃思医生问。

“您愿意就去。开门的时候，千万不要弄出一点声响，而且请把门推开的最大幅度控制在一英寸之内。”

“我不会的，中尉。”

沃思医生觉得自己活脱脱像童年时在芬尼莫尔·库柏书里读到过的那种传奇人物，他回到了恩迪科特的房间。

瓦尔考中尉又整整等了一分钟，才打开门走进去。他没有看恩迪科特夫人，而是轻手轻脚地走到一把椅子前，把它提起来，摆在紧靠床边的位置。他从口袋里掏出那只鞋，坐了下来。

屋里是一派彻头彻尾的死寂。整整三分钟——他借着腕表给自己计时——他一动不动地坐着，凝望着恩迪科特夫人那合着的眼睑。

随后，他开始把那只鞋轻轻的、却又锲而不捨、带着一种富有节奏的规律性，在椅子的一只扶手上敲了起来。

哒——哒——哒——哒——哒——

恩迪科特夫人的脸，仍保持着睡眠中那种光洁的无表情。

哒——哒——哒——

她的呼吸，仍稳稳地维持在睡眠的深处。

哒——哒——哒——哒——

“你要是再这样敲下去，”她平静地开口，“我会发疯的。”

## XXIII 4:29 a.m.—A Turn of the Screw / 二十三 凌晨四时二十九分——再拧一圈螺丝

===Original===
“You needn’t say anything you don’t care to, Mrs. Endicott.”

“I’m glad you didn’t use the stereotyped formula, Lieutenant. It would have disappointed me if you had. Get me a cigarette, please; there are some over there on the dresser.”

Lieutenant Valcour stood up. He got the cigarettes and lighted one for Mrs. Endicott and one for himself.

“You shouldn’t have dropped your slipper outside of the window,” he said.

===Chinese===
“您不想说的话，尽可以不说，恩迪科特夫人。”

“您没用那句千篇一律的套话，我很高兴，中尉。您若是用了，我反倒要失望。劳驾递支烟给我；梳妆台上就有。”

瓦尔考中尉站起身来。他取过烟，给恩迪科特夫人点了一支，自己也点了一支。

“您本不该把拖鞋掉在窗外的。”他说。

===Original===
“You shouldn’t have found it.”

Her eyes, now that they were opened, were admirably guarded, and her fingers, as they held the cigarette, showed no trace of nervousness.

“The slipper is of no great consequence, Mrs. Endicott. There are so many other things, too, you see.”

“Sort of a wholesale strewing of clues? I never imagined you as bothering very much with clues. It’s people you’re more interested in: reading their minds.”

Her eyes offered an almost impudent invitation that he read hers.

===Chinese===
“您本不该找着它的。”

她的眼睛此刻既已睁开，便守得滴水不漏，叫人暗暗称奇；她夹着烟的手指，也看不出一丝紧张的痕迹。

“这只拖鞋算不得什么了不得的东西，恩迪科特夫人。您瞧，此外还有那么多别的呢。”

“算是成把成把地抛线索咯？我从没想到您会这么计较线索。您更有兴趣的是人：读他们的心思。”

她的眼神近乎放肆地邀他去读她自己的心思。

===Original===
“Whom were you aiming at when you fired, Mrs. Endicott, at your husband or at Mr. Hollander?”

Mrs. Endicott blew smoke rings elaborately.

“At neither, Lieutenant. I didn’t have a gun.”

“Then it was just curiosity?”

“What was?”

“Your going out on the balcony.”

“I didn’t go out on the balcony. I’ve never been on it in my life.”

“I am not stupid, Mrs. Endicott.”

“Nor very credulous, either.”

“No, nor credulous.”

“That’s the trouble with truth: it often sounds so silly.”

===Chinese===
“您开枪的时候，瞄的是谁，恩迪科特夫人？是您丈夫，还是霍兰德先生（Mr. Hollander）？”

恩迪科特夫人不慌不忙地吐出一串烟圈。

“哪个都不是，中尉。我手里没枪。”

“那仅仅是出于好奇？”

“什么好奇？”

“您走到阳台上去。”

“我没有走到阳台上去。我这辈子就没上过那阳台。”

“我并不蠢，恩迪科特夫人。”

“也不算很轻信。”

“对，不轻信。”

“这正是真相的麻烦之处：它听着往往蠢透了。”

===Original===
“Surely you realize how things look against you, Mrs. Endicott.”

“Black.”

“The worst of all is your not having taken the narcotic, and then having pretended to be in a state of unconsciousness.”

Her eyes became stupefyingly innocent. “Is it illegal to decide not to take medicine, Lieutenant?”

His respect for her as an adversary began to mount by leaps and bounds. “No, Mrs. Endicott. But in the present case it was purposefully deceptive.”

“Why, I simply disliked hurting Dr. Worth’s feelings; that was all.”

===Chinese===
“您总该明白，事到如今种种迹象对您是何等不利，恩迪科特夫人。”

“一团漆黑。”

“最糟糕的一点，是您没有服下那剂麻醉药，却又装作不省人事。”

她的眼神变得天真得令人发噤。“决定不吃药，这也犯法吗，中尉？”

他视她为对手的敬意，开始飞速地节节攀升。“不犯法，恩迪科特夫人。但在眼下的案子里，这是存心的欺瞒。”

“哎呀，我不过是舍不得拂了沃思医生的面子；仅此而已。”

===Original===
Lieutenant Valcour pictured her maintaining that attitude—smartly dressed in becomingly plain black, very innocent, very beautiful-looking—before the twelve impressionable and normally dumb people one finds on juries. He was grudgingly afraid she could get away with it.

“And it isn’t illegal, either,” she went on, “to go to sleep, is it?”

Lieutenant Valcour decided that if anything was to be gained from the interview he would have to give a turn to the screw.

“No, Mrs. Endicott, sleeping isn’t illegal. Even,” he added negligently, “if your husband has just been killed, and your—well, whatever state of relationship exists between you and Mr. Hollander—your friend, let us say, is wounded to the point of death.”

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉在脑中描摹她维持着这副姿态的样子——一身剪裁得体、素净中见雅致的黑色衣装，一脸天真，容颜极美——站在十二名易受左右、平日多半沉默寡言的那种陪审员面前。他心有不甘地担心，她说不定真能全身而退。

“再者，睡着也不犯法，”她接着说，“不是吗？”

瓦尔考中尉断定，若想从这场问话里榨出半点收获，他就不得不把螺丝再拧上一圈。

“不犯法，恩迪科特夫人，睡着不犯法。哪怕，”他漫不经心地补上一句，“您的丈夫刚刚送了命，而您那位——嗯，无论您和霍兰德先生之间是何种关系——您那位朋友，姑且这么说吧，已经伤得只剩半条命了。”

===Original===
The cigarette dropped from her fingers to the floor. Lieutenant Valcour crushed it with the sole of his shoe.

“I don’t believe you.”

Her voice had the same pallid qualities as her skin.

“You must have seen for yourself, Mrs. Endicott, that he was pretty badly hurt when he slipped to the floor. There was blood enough smeared around, goodness knows.”

“You’re trying to trap me.”

“Just stating facts, Mrs. Endicott. Of course you may have left the instant after you fired and so not have seen Mr. Hollander shot down by the police.”

“You are being vulgarly brutal.”

===Chinese===
香烟从她指间滑落，掉到地上。瓦尔考中尉用鞋底把它碾熄。

“我不信您。”

她的嗓音和她那肤色一样，带着一种苍白的质地。

“您想必亲眼看见了，恩迪科特夫人，他滑倒在地时伤得不轻。天知道，四下抹得到处都是血。”

“您是想给我下套。”

“不过是陈述事实罢了，恩迪科特夫人。当然，您也可能在开枪之后那一瞬便抽身走了，因而没瞧见霍兰德先生被警方击倒。”

“您粗鄙得近乎残忍。”

===Original===
“You were certainly in a frantic enough hurry to have dropped your slipper and not to have bothered to pick it up. Did you throw the gun into the garden, Mrs. Endicott? We’re bound to find it, you know.”

“Is Mr. Hollander still in the house?”

“No.”

“Where have they taken him?”

“To the hospital.”

“Please ring for my maid and leave the room. I must go to him immediately.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Will you please leave this room?”

“You don’t seem to realize, Mrs. Endicott, that you are under arrest.”

===Chinese===
“您当时那副慌不择路的样子，确是急得连拖鞋掉了、也顾不上俯身去捡的。您是把枪扔进花园了吗，恩迪科特夫人？您知道，我们迟早会找到它的。”

“霍兰德先生还在屋里吗？”

“不在了。”

“他们把他送哪儿去了？”

“送去了医院。”

“劳驾按铃叫我的女佣，然后离开这间房。我必须立刻到他那儿去。”

“很抱歉。”

“请您离开这间房，行吗？”

“您似乎还没意识到，恩迪科特夫人，您已经被捕了。”

===Original===
The thought stunned her. Her head fell back among the pillows as if it had been thrown there.

“But that’s silly—silly, I tell you.”

“You admitted yourself, Mrs. Endicott, that the truth is always silly.”

“You are actually charging me with the murder of my husband?”

“‘Arrest’ was perhaps an injudicious word. I am holding you, Mrs. Endicott, as a material witness, for the present.”

Mrs. Endicott had recovered somewhat from the shock.

“I shan’t be bromidic, Lieutenant, and attempt either tears or bribery. I’m not stupid enough to think that either would affect you in the slightest from the performance of duty. But I should like to appeal to your reason.”

===Chinese===
这个念头把她击懵了。她的头颓然倒回枕头里，像是被人摔进去似的。

“可这太蠢了——我跟您说，太蠢了。”

“是您自己承认的，恩迪科特夫人，真相向来都是蠢的。”

“您当真要以谋杀亲夫的罪名控告我？”

“‘逮捕’这个词或许用得欠妥。眼下我只是把您当作重要证人（material witness）暂行看押，恩迪科特夫人。”

恩迪科特夫人已从那一震之下稍稍缓了过来。

“我不会落俗套的，中尉，既不打算哭，也不打算行贿。我还没蠢到以为这两样能叫您在履职时有半分动摇。不过，我倒想诉诸您的理性。”

===Original===
“You will find me a sympathetic listener, Mrs. Endicott. My wretched conceit forces me to add that I shall also be an intelligent one.”

“You see, I knew pretty well what was going on from hearing the nurse and Roberts talking about it. Lieutenant, just what do you want me to admit?”

“That you were on the balcony.”

“But I wasn’t.”

“Then how did your slipper get there?”

“It fell from my foot.”

Lieutenant Valcour stood up abruptly. “You will have to pardon me, Mrs. Endicott,” he said, “while I search this room.”

“You misunderstand me. I mean exactly what I say. I wasn’t on the balcony, and the slipper did fall off my foot. If you must know it, I was straddling the window sill.”

===Chinese===
“您会发觉我是位富有同情的听众，恩迪科特夫人。我这点可悲的自负逼着我再补一句：我也会是位听得懂话的听众。”

“您瞧，我听见那位护士（the nurse）和罗伯茨谈这事，差不多就知道出了什么。中尉，您到底想让我承认什么？”

“承认您当时在阳台上。”

“可我并不在。”

“那您的拖鞋是怎么到那儿的？”

“它从我脚上掉了下去。”

瓦尔考中尉猛地站起身。“您得恕我失礼，恩迪科特夫人，”他说，“我得搜一搜这间房。”

“您误会我的意思了。我一个字也不多、一个字不少。我不在阳台上，拖鞋也的确是从我脚上掉下去的。您非要刨根问底的话——我那会儿正跨坐在窗台上。”

===Original===
“What stopped you from going out, Mrs. Endicott?”

“The sound of the shooting. It unnerved me. I almost fell back into the room and closed the window. I knew that I had dropped a slipper outside, but the idea of doing anything further than hurrying back into bed terrified me.”

Lieutenant Valcour examined the slipper he still held in his hand. “This is a slipper for the left foot,” he said. “And in that case, when you were straddling the window it is the foot which must have been on the outside. Isn’t that so?”

“That’s rather elementary, isn’t it?”

“Quite. But it serves to prove that at the moment when the shots were fired you could look along the balcony toward the windows of your husband’s room. Did you?”

“I imagine so. I’m not quite certain, really. It was absolutely dark out there.”

===Chinese===
“那是什么拦住了您没出去，恩迪科特夫人？”

“是开枪的声音。它叫我撑不住了。我差点跌回屋里，赶紧把窗关上。我知道自己把一只拖鞋掉在了外头，可一想到除了赶紧钻回床上之外的任何举动，都叫我怕得不行。”

瓦尔考中尉端详着仍拿在手里的那只拖鞋。“这是左脚的拖鞋，”他说，“既如此，您跨坐窗台的时候，在外头的那只脚必是左脚。对吗？”

“这未免太浅显了吧，不是吗？”

“不错。可这恰好证明：开枪的那一刻，您是能沿着阳台望向您丈夫房间那几扇窗户的。您望了吗？”

“我猜望过吧。说真的，我并没十分把握。外头那时黑极了。”

===Original===
“On the contrary, there was a glow cast on the balcony from the farthest window, which was open a little, wasn’t there?”

“Perhaps. Yes, I think there was.”

“And did you see anybody standing at that window when the shots were fired?”

“You mean on the balcony?”

“Yes.”

“No.”

“That is all, Mrs. Endicott.”

“You don’t believe me.”

“Frankly, I don’t.”

===Chinese===
“恰恰相反，最远那扇窗开着一条缝，有一道微光投在阳台上，不是吗？”

“也许吧。是的，我想是有的。”

“那开枪的时候，您可曾看见什么人站在那扇窗前？”

“您是指在阳台上？”

“对。”

“没有。”

“问完了，恩迪科特夫人。”

“您不相信我。”

“坦白讲，不相信。”

===Original===
Mrs. Endicott’s expression hardened perceptibly. Whether from bitterness or from some sudden private determination it was difficult to say.

“Does being detained as a material witness prohibit me from getting out of bed and dressing?” she said.

“Not at all. In fact, it is essential that you do so. You see, we detain our material witnesses in jail.”

He heard again, as he had heard it earlier in the night, the muted echo of brass bells in her voice. “If you will leave me then, please?”

“Just as soon as I have searched the room.”

“For what?”

“For a revolver, Mrs. Endicott.”

===Chinese===
恩迪科特夫人的神情肉眼可见地僵了僵。究竟是出于酸楚，还是出于某种突如其来的、私下的决断，倒难说得很。

“作为重要证人被看押，就不准我下床穿衣了吗？”她说。

“一点也不。事实上，您非这么做不可。要知道，我们把重要证人都拘在看守所里。”

他又一次听见——一如前半夜曾听见过的那样——她嗓音里那抹压低了声的铜铃回响。“那么，请让我一个人待着，行吗？”

“等我把这间房搜完就走。”

“搜什么？”

“搜一把左轮手枪，恩迪科特夫人。”

===Original===
Mrs. Endicott closed her eyes. She turned on her side and faced the wall. Lieutenant Valcour conducted his search with the thoroughness and speed born of experience. In the room, in the room’s cupboard, in the various drawers, beneath the different pieces of furniture, there was no gun. He took a dressing gown and placed it on the bed.

“Put this on, please, Mrs. Endicott, I want to search the bed.”

She did so, without either comment or objection. She went to the window and stared unseeingly at the breaking day.

Lieutenant Valcour removed the spread, and with a pencil roughly outlined the damp spot where the narcotic had been spilled. Then he folded the spread and tucked it under one arm. The rest of the bedclothes, the mattress, the pillows, concealed no gun. He walked to the door.

“I will send your maid to you, Mrs. Endicott, if you wish.”

===Chinese===
恩迪科特夫人闭上了眼。她侧过身去，面朝墙躺着。瓦尔考中尉以经验养成的周全与利落展开了搜查。屋里、屋里的衣橱里、各个抽屉里、各件家具底下，都没有枪。他取来一件晨衣，搁到床上。

“请把这个披上，恩迪科特夫人，我要搜一搜床。”

她照办了，既无评论，也无异议。她走到窗前，望着外面那正在破晓的天色，却视而不见。

瓦尔考中尉掀开床罩，用一支铅笔粗粗勾勒出麻醉剂泼洒出的那块湿痕。然后他把床罩叠起，挟在臂弯里。余下的床上用品、褥垫、枕头，都藏不住枪。他朝门口走去。

“您要是愿意，恩迪科特夫人，我这就叫您的女佣过来。”

===Original===
She continued to stare through the window and to present her back to him. She said nothing. He tried to catch the suggestion in her pose. It wasn’t a gesture of petty rudeness or angry spite; nor was it by any means suggestive of despair or fear. He went outside and closed the door.

And as he crossed the corridor to Endicott’s room it occurred to him with shocking clearness that, in spite of the idea’s seeming absurdity, her pose had suggested a very definite mood of positive exaltation.

===Chinese===
她仍旧望着窗外，把背脊留给他。一言不发。他试图捕捉她这姿态里暗藏的意味。那既不是小家子气的无礼、也不是愠怒的怨毒；更说不上是绝望或恐惧。他走出门外，带上了门。

就在他穿过走廊、朝恩迪科特的房间走去时，一个念头以一种令人心惊的清晰涌上心头：尽管这想法听着荒诞，可她那姿态分明透出一种确凿的、近乎激越的昂扬之情。

## XXIV 4:41 a.m.—As the Colours of Dawn / 二十四 凌晨四时四十一分——如黎明的诸般色彩

===Original===
“Well,” Lieutenant Valcour said, as he joined Dr. Worth in Endicott’s room, “what do you think now?”

Dr. Worth was finished with bewilderments. In spite of the camel’s-hair robe swathing him, he had recaptured to an impressive extent his air of dignity.

“Lieutenant,” he said, “I think that my services are no longer required in this house. With your permission, I shall dismiss the two nurses and go home.”

“Why, certainly, Doctor, if you wish. The prosecuting attorney will probably require your testimony to secure an indictment and will want you later on at the trial, but I’m sure he will bother you just as little as possible. We realize how annoying any court work is to a doctor.”

“I shall be glad to testify whenever required.”

“Will you also let me know where to keep in touch with the two nurses? Their testimony will be needed, too.”

===Chinese===
“那么，”瓦尔考中尉走进恩迪科特的房间，与沃思医生（Dr. Worth）会合时说道，“您现在作何想法？”

沃思医生已再无惊愕可言。尽管身上仍裹着那件驼毛晨袍，他倒已大程度地重新拾回了自己那股气度。

“中尉，”他说，“我想我这宅子里已用不着我的本事了。您若允许，我便把那两名护士遣散，自己回家去了。”

“哦，那自然可以，医生，您请便。检察官那边，要拿到大陪审团的起诉书多半还要您出庭作证，到了正式开庭审案时怕也免不了再麻烦您一次，可我敢说他一定会尽可能少来搅扰您。法庭上那一摊子差事对一位医生有多恼人，我们都心里有数。”

“什么时候需要我作证，我都乐意效劳。”

“还有那两名护士，您能不能也告诉我，我上哪儿能随时联络到她们？她们的证词往后也要用得上。”

===Original===
Dr. Worth stated the name and address of the Nurses’ Home at which Miss Vickers and Miss Murrow could always be reached, and Lieutenant Valcour wrote them down in his notebook.

“Would it bother you very much, Lieutenant, to let Mrs. Endicott know that I have gone, when you see her?”

“Not at all, Doctor.”

“I doubt whether she will require my services again.” He paused for a moment at the doorway. “That woman, sir, is of iron.”

“I shouldn’t wonder, Doctor. At any rate, she is pretty thoroughly encased in metal. I’ll send Cassidy along with you to pass you and the nurses by O’Brian down at the door. No one can leave the house, you see, without permission.”

“Thank you, Lieutenant. Goodbye.”

“Goodbye, Doctor, and thanks for all your assistance. Cassidy, come back after you’ve seen the doctor out, and stay in the corridor. I’ll call when I need you.”

“Yes, sir.”

===Chinese===
沃思医生报出了维克斯小姐（Miss Vickers）和默罗小姐（Miss Murrow）随时都能被找到的那所护士宿舍的名字与地址，瓦尔考中尉把它们一一记进了笔记本里。

“中尉，您见到恩迪科特夫人的时候，劳您驾替我知会她一声说我已经走了，会不会太麻烦您？”

“一点也不，医生。”

“我估摸着她不会再需要我做什么了。”他在门口停了片刻。“先生，那个女人，是铁打的。”

“我不奇怪，医生。无论如何，她浑身上下可算是被铁皮裹了个严严实实。我这就派卡西迪（Cassidy）陪您下去，好把您和两位护士送到门口，让奥布赖恩（O’Brian）放行。您要知道，没有得到许可，谁也不得离开这宅子。”

“多谢您，中尉。再会。”

“再会，医生，多谢您一路的协助。卡西迪，送医生出去之后再回来，在走廊里候着。我有事会叫你。”

“是，长官。”

===Original===
The door closed, and Lieutenant Valcour was alone. With a persistence that was becoming annoying, the same curious feeling of lurking danger crept out at him from the room’s stillnesses. His nerves were usually as steady as the quality reputed to be enjoyed by a rock, and the strange little jumpings they were going in for were getting that fabulous animal known as his goat.

He went over to the chair before the flat-topped desk and sat down. There was that drawer filled with disordered papers to be gone through. He removed the drawer and emptied it of its contents by the simple expedient of turning it upside down onto the top of the desk.

There were, mixed up among bills and receipts, a surprising number of letters from women. He read each one of them carefully and felt a little sorrier, at the conclusion of each, for the future of the race—not so much because of any danger to its morals as to its mentality.

===Chinese===
门关上了，瓦尔考中尉独自留在了屋里。那同一种古怪的、仿佛有什么祸患正潜伏其中的感觉，带着一股叫人恼火的执拗，又从房间的种种寂静里朝他爬了出来。他的神经素来稳如磐石——据说磐石便享有这等品性——而这会儿它们却不安地一阵阵小跳，正惹得那头名唤“他的脾气”的传说中的怪兽渐渐发作起来。

他走到那张平顶书桌前的椅子旁，坐了下来。那只塞满了凌乱文件的书桌抽屉，还得由他翻检一遍。他把抽屉整个儿抽出来，索性把它倒扣在桌面上，里面的东西便一股脑儿倾了出来。

在一堆账单和收据之间，混着数量多得惊人的女人来信。他把每一封都细细读过，每读完一封，心里便为人类的将来多添几分悲凉——倒不全是为其道德之忧，更多是为其智识之忧。

===Original===
He made a little group of each batch of notes from the same woman. One pile topped the list with the number of ten. These were signed “Bebe” and were addressed with deplorable monotony to “My cave man.” Endicott must have been rather an ass, he decided, as well as a pretty low sort of an animal. It was all very well for Roberts to rave on about soldiers, and simple hearts, and war, and things. That’s just what it amounted to: raving. What if Endicott and, presumably, her brother had had simple hearts. So had guinea pigs.

Lieutenant Valcour wondered whether everyone else connected with the case was quite sane and he just a little mad. Roberts—Mrs. Endicott—the housekeeper—Hollander—Madame Velasquez. They all seemed a little touched, and that was a sign of madness when one considered everyone else but one’s self insane. But no one was ever truly normal under disagreeable and terrifying circumstances; at least, he had never found anyone who was so.

The letters were meaningless as possible clues to a motive; just a sticky conglomeration of lust, greed, dullness, and execrable taste. He shoved them aside.

===Chinese===
他把同一个女人寄来的每一批信件各自归成一小摞。其中有一摞以整整十封的数目高居榜首。它们署名“蓓蓓（Bebe）”，称谓则单调得令人发指，清一色写着“我的穴居男人”。他暗自断定，恩迪科特不但是个十足的蠢驴，还是品性相当卑下的畜生。罗伯茨尽管在那里大发议论，扯什么军人啦、纯朴的心啦、战争啦、诸如此类。那番话究其实，不过是癫狂之语。就算恩迪科特，多半还有她那位哥哥，当真都有一颗纯朴的心，那又怎样。豚鼠也有。

瓦尔考中尉纳闷，是不是所有与这桩案子沾边的人都神志健全，唯独他自己有几分疯癫。罗伯茨——恩迪科特夫人——那位管家——霍兰德——委拉斯开兹夫人。他们一个个都似乎有几分不对劲，而当一个人把除自己以外的所有人都视作疯子时，那便是自己疯了的一种征兆。可话又说回来，身处在这样叫人难堪、叫人惊惧的境地里，谁也不可能真正做到正常如常；至少，他从未见过有谁还能如此。

这些信件作为动机的线索来看，毫无意义可言；不过是欲望、贪婪、乏味和糟糕透顶的趣味，黏糊糊地搅成一团。他把它们统统推到一边。

===Original===
He watched the strengthening light of day as it came through the window across the desk before him. Such sky as he saw was of rubbed emerald, and the backs of the houses across the intervening gardens were mauve and dark gray, with lines of lemon yellow running thinly along their roofs.

He thought of *Bohême*—dawn always made him think of *Bohême*—and hummed a bar or two of it softly. Then he thought of Mrs. Endicott, and his thoughts were pastelled in the colours of the dawn: a woman of halftones and overlapping lacquer shades.

It became quite clear in his mind that she never would have killed her husband. Or Hollander. That, in fact, she never would have killed anybody at all. The belief became fixed, even in face of the sizeable amount of evidence against her.

===Chinese===
他望着窗外一寸寸亮起来的天光，看它透过窗子洒到他面前的书桌上。他所能望见的那一线天幕，是揉磨过的翡翠色，隔着中间几座花园的对面那一排房屋的背面，则呈紫红与深灰，屋顶上淡淡地勾着一线柠檬黄。

他想起了《波希米亚人》（*Bohême*）——黎明总叫他想起《波希米亚人》——便低低地哼了其中一两小节。随后他又想起了恩迪科特夫人，而他对她的种种念头，都被黎明的颜色染成了柔和的粉彩：一个由半调子与层层叠叠的漆色阴影构成的女人。

在他心里，这事已经变得相当清楚：她绝不会杀她丈夫。也不会杀霍兰德。事实上，她根本就不会杀任何人。纵然面前摆着相当分量对她不利的证据，这个信念却愈发地笃定下来。

===Original===
He reviewed her case, in digest, as the prosecuting attorney might present it to a jury: from the very start there was that contrary fact of her having telephoned for the police. Why? On the slender ground of a pencilled note that might or might not have been a threat, and an instinctive premonition that her husband was in danger. The prosecution would thereupon interpolate a smart crack or two on the general subject of premonitions, fortune tellings, and the Ace of Spades. They would point out that people who committed crimes which were bound to be shortly discovered occasionally got in touch with the police in order to use the gesture as a premise of their innocence.

There were her definite admissions of intent to kill her husband—her having left her bedroom immediately upon his having knocked and said goodbye—and her recent most damaging actions in regard to the narcotic and having been on the balcony.

Motive?

The prosecuting attorney could offer a thousand. The most prominent ones would include a jealous rage at her husband’s easily proved peccadillos with other women and her own rather significant attitude toward Hollander. Yes, it would be only too possible for the prosecuting attorney to get a conviction against Mrs. Endicott, and to rope Hollander in as an accomplice. He’d want the weapon, though, to make the case complete. Lieutenant Valcour had forgotten about the weapon. He stood up, went to the door, and opened it. Hansen was standing outside, having taken his post there until Cassidy should come back from letting out Dr. Worth and the nurses.

===Chinese===
他像检察官将来当庭向陪审团陈词那般，把她的案子提纲挈领地在心里过了一遍：打一开始，便有那样一个与公诉口径相左的事实——她竟然主动打电话报了警。为什么？所凭的不过是那么一张铅笔写的纸条——是不是恐吓还两说——再加上一种她丈夫身处险境的本能预感。控方必然会就此插进几句俏皮话，就预感啦、算命占卜啦、黑桃A啦这些话题泛泛地挖苦一番。他们会指出，有些作案之人，明知罪行不久必将败露，偏还偶尔去与警方搭上线，无非是拿这一举动当作证明自己清白的前提。

她有过明白无误的承认，流露过要杀丈夫的意图——他来敲门道别之后，她当即离开了卧室——再加上近来那几桩最能定她罪的行为：关于那安眠药的，以及她出现在阳台上的那一桩。

动机？

检察官能举出一千条来。最显眼的几条，无外乎她丈夫与别的女人那些轻易便可坐实的风流勾当所惹起的醋性大发作，以及她自己那对霍兰德意味颇为深长的态度。是的，要让检察官把恩迪科特夫人定罪，再捎带把霍兰德以从犯之名一齐收罗进来，那是再容易不过。可要把这案子办成铁案，他还得有那把凶器。瓦尔考中尉这才想起凶器的事。他站起身，走到门边，把门打开。汉森（Hansen）正守在门外——卡西迪送沃思医生和两位护士出去之后尚未回来，他便暂替了这处岗位。

===Original===
“Hansen,” Lieutenant Valcour said, “I want you to search the backyard for a revolver that may have been thrown there from the balcony. If you can’t find it, search the two adjoining backyards, and the three in the rear as well. Don’t wake up the people in the other houses, just get a stepladder and cross the party walls.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Report to me as soon as you’ve finished, or find anything.”

“Yes, sir.”

Lieutenant Valcour closed the door again. The revolver would clinch the case: Mrs. Endicott the principal, and Hollander the accomplice. What a sweet bunch of muck it would be, too. There were all sorts of sob angles: Hollander and Endicott as Damon and Pythias, brothers in arms during the war who were transformed through the vicious caprice of a siren into Cain and Abel. Or would Mrs. Endicott spatter the tabloids as a woman wronged who had by a reversal of the usual position of the sexes taken her just revenge beneath the legendary cloak of the unwritten law? If her lawyers were smart, she would. And they would be smart, too. She’d probably have the most impressive battery of legal guns that were procurable in the state lined up on her side.

===Chinese===
“汉森，”瓦尔考中尉说，“我要你去搜后院，找一把也许从阳台上扔下去的左轮手枪。要是找不到，再去搜两边紧挨着的两户后院，还有再往后那三户的也别落下。别吵醒别家的人，找一架活梯，翻过共用的隔墙过去便是。”

“是，长官。”

“一搜完，或者一有所发现，就立刻向我回报。”

“是，长官。”

瓦尔考中尉重新带上了门。那把左轮手枪能把案子钉成铁案：恩迪科特夫人为首犯，霍兰德为从犯。到时那一摊子烂泥，可真要乱得够瞧。煽情的路数五花八门：霍兰德与恩迪科特，本来情同莫逆，是战时并肩的袍泽，却被一个妖女恶毒的一时任性，生生变作了该隐与亚伯。又或者，恩迪科特夫人会不会以一个蒙冤女子的姿态溅满各路小报——说她反男女之位而行之，在那层名为“不成文法”的传奇外衣之下，行了她理所应当的报复？若她的律师精明，她会的。而他们也必会精明。她这一头，多半会请得动本州所能罗致的最显赫的一排法律大炮，齐刷刷为她列阵。

===Original===
It wasn’t the gun only that Lieutenant Valcour wanted. There was something else. Endicott’s hat: that was it. How did the person who had been caught in the cupboard fit in with Endicott’s hat? The answer came to him with the sudden clearness that will enlighten a problem that the subconscious mind has been working on for some time. The hat was the final touch to the person’s disguise. And the fact would presuppose a woman. A man’s hat would add immeasurably to any disguise adopted by a woman.

But which woman?

And why had his hat been in the cupboard?

And still there was no answer to the baffling question as to what had been the object of the search through Endicott’s pockets and his papers. There was, of course, a perfectly plain and logically possible solution: the object or paper, whatever it was, had been found and had been carried off by the thief along with Endicott’s hat and the top button from his overcoat. And if such were the case, just what that object or paper was might never be known.

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉想要的，还不止那把枪。还有别的东西。恩迪科特的帽子：就是这个。那个被堵在衣橱里的人，与恩迪科特的帽子之间，究竟是什么关系？答案陡然间明明白白地浮上了心头——那种突然的豁然开朗，总会在某个已被潜意识反复盘算了许久的问题上降临。那顶帽子，正是那人乔装打扮的最后一笔。而这一层又势必意味着，那是个女人。一顶男式帽子，能让女人任何一种乔装的效果，增色得不可估量。

可究竟是哪一个女人？

还有，他的帽子又为何会在那衣橱里？

至于那桩至今仍令人困惑不解的事——搜遍恩迪科特衣袋与文件，所寻的究竟是什么物件——仍旧没有答案。当然，一个清清楚楚、逻辑上也说得通的解答是现成摆在那里的：那物件或文件，无论是什么，早已被搜的人寻到，连同恩迪科特的帽子和他大衣最上面那颗纽扣一道被顺手带走了。倘若当真如此，那么那物件或文件究竟是什么，恐怕永远也无从知晓了。

===Original===
For the fourth time since he had been sitting at the desk Lieutenant Valcour sniffed the air. There was a faint trace of scent—a curiously reminiscent odour—all but intangible, but which he was quite certain he had encountered in some different locality at some time during the night. It was only apparent when he sat at the desk, and the deduction was reached without too much mental labour that it must, hence, emanate from something connected with the desk. Perhaps that aperture from which he had pulled the drawer—

The telephone rang sharply. He drew the instrument to him across the top of the desk, and took the receiver from the hook.

The call came, he was informed, from Central Office.

===Chinese===
这是他坐在书桌前以来，第四次去嗅空气里的味道。有一丝若有若无的香气——一种古怪地引人追忆的气味——几乎摸不着也辨不清，可他相当有把握，夜里某个时辰、在另一处地方，他曾经闻到过它。唯有他坐在这张书桌前时，这气味才浮现出来；由此推出的结论并不太费脑筋：它必定是从与这张书桌相关的什么东西上散发出来的。也许是那只他刚才抽出抽屉的开口处——

电话骤然响了起来。他把电话机顺着桌面拖到自己跟前，从挂钩上取下听筒。

他被告知，这通电话是从总局打来的。

## XXV 5:01 a.m.—Lunatic Vistas / 二十五 凌晨五时零一分——疯癫的诸般远景

===Original===
The report from Central Office which Lieutenant Valcour received over the telephone contained one definitely useful piece of information: the person who had used the comb and brushes belonging to Endicott had been a blonde and was either a man or a woman with bobbed hair.

And Mrs. Endicott, Lieutenant Valcour reflected as he hung up the receiver, had blonde shingled hair. And so, except for the shingling, did Hollander.

Roberts, on the other hand, had not.

And where, he wanted to know, was his inspiring confidence in the innocence of Mrs. Endicott now? Precisely where it had been before. His mind began to gibber. What *was* that curious scent, that trace of an aroma? What about Hollander’s roommate: the young Southerner who preyed upon wealthy women in night clubs? Had Endicott evidence that Hollander was mixed up in similar jobs, and had Hollander come to steal it, or silence Endicott? Rats! And what were Marge Myles’s address and telephone number doing in Mrs. Endicott’s personal directory? And why had Mrs. Endicott been such a stupid liar as to say she had seen no one on the balcony at the time when the shots were fired, when the only apparent place from which the shot that had killed Endicott could have been fired was the balcony? … A knock-knock.

===Chinese===
瓦尔考中尉从电话里接到的、由总局传来的那份报告，包含了唯一一条确实派得上用场的信息：用过恩迪科特那把梳子和发刷的人，是个金发，要么是个男人，要么是个剪短发的女人。

瓦尔考中尉一面挂上听筒，一面暗想：恩迪科特夫人正有一头剪短贴颈的金发。霍兰德也是金发——除了不贴颈剪短之外，倒是一模一样。

而罗伯茨，则不是。

他倒要知道，他先前那股令人振奋的、对恩迪科特夫人清白无辜的笃信，眼下跑到哪儿去了？原封不动地待在它原来待着的地方。他的脑子开始喋喋不休地说起胡话来。那股古怪的香气究竟是什么，那一丝若有若无的气味？霍兰德那个室友又怎么说：那个在夜总会里专猎阔绰女人的南方青年？恩迪科特手里是不是抓着霍兰德也卷入了同类勾当的证据，霍兰德这才赶过来偷走它，或者索性让恩迪科特永远开不了口？胡扯！玛吉·迈尔斯的住址和电话号码，又是怎么进了恩迪科特夫人那本私人通讯录的？恩迪科特夫人又何以蠢到要撒那样一个谎，说枪响之际她并未看见阳台上有任何人——可击毙恩迪科特的那一枪，唯一显而易见能够开火的所在，偏偏就是那阳台？……笃笃，有人敲门。

===Original===
“Come in,” he said.

Cassidy opened the door.

“There’s an old dame downstairs, Lieutenant, who insisted on coming in. She wants to see you.”

“Did she say who she was, Cassidy?”

“She did. And you can believe it or not, sir, but her name is Molasses.”

Lieutenant Valcour made a desperate clutch at his scattering reason.

“By all means, Cassidy,” he said, “show Mrs. Molasses right up.”

===Chinese===
“进来。”他说。

卡西迪推开了门。

“中尉，楼下有位老太太，非要进来不可。她要见您。”

“她报了姓名没有，卡西迪？”

“报了。长官，信不信由您，她姓‘莫拉塞斯’（Molasses，意为‘糖蜜’）。”

瓦尔考中尉死命攥住了他那四散奔逃的理智。

“务必请她上来，卡西迪，”他说，“把莫拉塞斯太太直接请上来。”

===Original===
Madame Velasquez, in the penetrating light of early morning, was beyond words. The intervening hours since Lieutenant Valcour had left her, wigless and talking to herself in her stepdaughter’s apartment, had unquestionably been ones of worry. As she came into the room Lieutenant Valcour motioned to Cassidy to wait outside and close the corridor door.

Over her black sequinned dress she had thrown an evening cape of blue satin edged with marabou, and on her wig rested a picture hat trimmed with plumes. Her eyes ignored the details of Endicott’s room, of Endicott’s body stretched beneath the sheet; ignored everything but Lieutenant Valcour, the man whom she had come to see.

===Chinese===
清晨那道犀利的光线之下，委拉斯开兹夫人简直无法用言语形容。自从瓦尔考中尉丢下她——戴假发、独自在继女寓所里自言自语地喃喃——离去之后，这几个钟头里，她无疑一直忧心如焚。她一走进屋子，瓦尔考中尉便朝卡西迪示意，叫他在外头等候，并随手关上走廊那扇门。

她在那身缀满亮片的黑衣裙外面，胡乱罩了一件蓝缎晚斗篷，斗篷的边沿镶着一圈鹳绒毛；假发之上则搁着一顶缀着羽毛的宽檐阔边女帽。她的两眼全不在意恩迪科特房里的种种细节，全不在意那具被床单盖住、直挺挺躺着的恩迪科特的身子；除了瓦尔考中尉——她此来要见的那个男人——之外，旁的一切她一概视若无物。

===Original===
“Marge is dead,” she said.

Her voice still retained the curious qualities that made it suggest a scream.

Lieutenant Valcour wearily closed his eyes. One other murder would truly prove to be the straw with himself in the role of the already overladen camel.

“Sit down, Madame Velasquez,” he said, “and tell me how it happened.”

Madame Velasquez spread billows of blue satin and marabou into an armchair.

“I don’t know how it happened,” she said.

===Chinese===
“玛吉死了。”她说。

她的嗓音仍保留着那种古怪的特质，听着总像是某声尖叫的前兆。

瓦尔考中尉疲倦地阖上双眼。再添上一桩命案，那可真要成为压垮骆驼的最后一根稻草了——而他自己，便是那头早已不堪重负的骆驼。

“请坐，委拉斯开兹夫人，”他说，“把事情的原委告诉我。”

委拉斯开兹夫人把层层叠叠的蓝缎与鹳绒，像浪涛一般铺展着泻进了一张扶手椅里。

“我不知道原委是什么。”她说。

===Original===
“Did you find her body in the apartment?”

“There ain’t no body.” Madame Velasquez then added, as her brittle little eyes glittered with a strange sort of conviction, “He made away with it.”

“Who did, Madame Velasquez?”

“Herbert Endicott,” she said.

===Chinese===
“您是在那寓所里寻着了她的尸首？”

“压根儿没有什么尸首。”委拉斯开兹夫人随即又补上一句，那双冷酷的小眼睛闪烁着一种古怪的笃信，“是他把尸首毁了。”

“是谁干的，委拉斯开兹夫人？”

“赫伯特·恩迪科特。”她说。

===Original===
For a startled moment Lieutenant Valcour stared sharply down curious vistas: *had* Endicott killed Marge Myles, perhaps having called for her just after she had written that note to her mother?
